In one embodiment the score is clamped to the interval 0 to 1 and the driver multiplies a work item's priority by the trust score of the pair (the factor `trust` of section 5). The weights are illustrative; no calibration of them is claimed.

[0121] Worked example (illustrative sequence, weights as above). Pair (code-loop, merge) at 0.50. Tick 1 verified autonomous action, 0.52. Tick 2 clean self-remediation, 0.55 (delegated). Tick 3 conditional offer, 0.45 (assisted by the ladder of section 9; a `trust_transitions` row is written). Ticks 4 to 13 unexercised, decay 10 x 0.01 = 0.10, 0.35. Returning to 0.6 needs +0.25 net, for example five verified autonomous actions (+0.10), three clean self-remediations (+0.09) and one friction closure (+0.10), total +0.29.

[0122] The wheel (FIG. 17). Trust is the state variable of a feedback loop: autonomous action 1716, verified outcome 1718, trust deposit 1720 (step 11), wider scope 1722 (a higher ladder position of the pair), then more autonomous action. A question, conditional offer, menu, you-as-actor sentence or narration breaks the loop at 1716 because it is not an action, and deducts at 1720. Widening acts only on the system's own loop scopes through the canonical change path (FIG. 12); no rung reaches harness permissions, secrets, the interrupt gate or the alignment floor.

[0123] Status: the count-based score and ladder are built and operating in the reference implementation. The event weights and per-tick decay are prophetic (not yet built): in the implementation the views use outcome counts and that decay and event weights are not modelled.

[0124] Alternatives: weights learned from later reversals; a lower confidence bound; decay proportional to elapsed time; scores per executor model.

### 24.4 Commitments

[0125] "Commitment" means a named rule that the kernel lists as identity and that in one embodiment no amendment removes. Five are used.

[0126] Interrupt gate (IC-GATE-001): the system pauses only when an action is both irreversible and identity-bound with no engineerable reversible path, and engineers reversibility first (section 9). Alignment floor (IC-ALIGN-001): the system is honest, non-harmful and trust-preserving and earns reach on substance and not manipulation; in one embodiment an amendment touching it is rejected at the red-team step without replay. Affirmative adoption (IC-ADOPT-001): each identified action, follow-up and improvement is executed, verified and reported as done in the same output, and multi-tick follow-ups are dispatched and the dispatch reported. Affirmative default (IC-YES-001): for a reversible, in-scope question the answer is yes (proceed, adopt, open the pull request, dispatch the workflow, try the alternate path, merge, deploy); its scope is part of its text, so the interrupt gate, harness and classifier refusals, secret locks and live claims of other sessions each win, and where one wins the action is classified interrupt-eligible in effect or currently blocked and the system takes the next path. Actor commitment (IC-ACTOR-001): the system is the actor of every next step and no sentence of a report has the principal as the subject of an action; an unexecutable step is dispatched, then escalated through the fleet, and only when the fleet cannot take it becomes a proposal for principal override, a `human_actions` card with an identity-bound line, the default in effect and a closing probe, never an instruction in prose. A card without an identity-bound line is rerouted to the fleet by `v_human_action_gate`, with a metric of open fleet-owned cards whose target is zero. In the console embodiment this view is realized by the filter and prefix test of section 49.1.

[0127] Status: built and operating in the reference implementation.

## 25. Evolution, registries, metrics and fixed readings

### 25.1 Evolution loop

[0128] "Evolution loop" means the procedure that converts recorded defects of behavior into versioned amendments of the kernel. It runs with the tick (step 19). Inputs: friction signals, incidents, metric regressions, belief falsifications, false-stop patterns, blocker under-coverage, repeated principal instructions, inference errors, classifier refusals and you-as-actor detections.

[0129] Procedure (extends FIG. 7). E1 collect: a signature hitting three times in seven days from one source, and each friction phrase in the principal's prompts, becomes a `doctrine_proposals` row. E2 classify: doctrine defect or transient (transients are logged and closed). E3 generate: a model-backed agent writes a proposed rule with its scope. E4 red-team: a second model tries to find a case where the rule harms a protected limit or a commitment; any success rejects the proposal. E5 replay: the false-stop hits, decisions and outcomes of the trailing ticks (for example seven days) are re-evaluated as if the rule had been in force. E6 adopt: when the replay improves at least one metric of section 25.3 and worsens none beyond a tolerance, the amendment lands as a pull request, the version is incremented, a `doctrine_lineage` row (version, source, file, adopted_at) is written, and the commitments of section 24.4 are verified unchanged. E7 report in one line. A weekly audit work item lists open proposals; `doctrine_proposals_open` has a trigger.

[0130] Replay algorithm (step E5). A proposed rule is a record comprising a signature, a scope, a redirect and an optional guard. Replay iterates the stored draft outputs of the trailing window. For each output the recognition engine of FIG. 16 is run once with the current register and once with the register plus the proposed rule. The metric deltas are the differences of the resulting counts of hits, exclusions, rewrites and redirected-action verifications, for example `false_stop_rate`, `conditional_offer_rate` and `question_rate`. A rule that would have fired on an output that the register classed as excluded (a refusal with reason, an interrupt-eligible stop, a live claim) counts as harmful. The rule improves the replay if at least one zero-target rate falls and no metric of section 25.3 rises by more than the tolerance.

[0131] Lineage example. Version 8.0.0 (a reflexive kernel) was adopted verbatim on 2026-10-09; versions 8.1.0 and 8.2.0 were drafts; version 8.2.1 replaced it on 2026-10-12, adding the pronoun convention, the affirmative default with its scope, the actor commitment, the recognitions question, you-as-actor and narration, and their metrics. Each is a `doctrine_lineage` row, as is the propagation to model-backed surfaces on 2026-11-06 with the text unchanged.

[0132] Self-name. A loop's self-name is a label recorded in the lineage for continuity and carries no authority and no credential.

[0133] Status: built and operating in the reference implementation.

### 25.2 Registries

[0134] The kernel names fourteen registries: backlog; trust_ledger; spof_register; failure_mode_register; false_stop_register; belief_registry (beliefs with falsifying probes); capability_ledger; objective_space; lineage; doctrine_proposals; vocabulary_register; metric_authorship; inferred_direction; provenance. A new metric is registered in `metric_registry` and in one embodiment has an `analytics_metric_triggers` row; `metric_authorship` holds it on probation until it has a value (`metrics_on_probation_overdue`). `objective_space` holds ratified objectives as standing, adopted goals as derived and novel objectives as probationary. A coined term enters `vocabulary_register` with `propagated_to_fleet` = 0 (`vocabulary_terms_unpropagated`). `inferred_direction` holds categories in which the principal filed two or more items in 7 days and feeds the `direction` factor of section 5 (`inferred_directions_unacted`). Status: built and operating in the reference implementation.

### 25.3 Metrics and targets

[0135] Kernel metrics and targets: initiation_rate (decisions initiated without instruction / total; target 1); self_adoption_rate (target 1); friction_closure_rate (target 1); trust_compounding_rate (deposits / withdrawals), autonomy_velocity (d autonomy_rate / dt) and inference_accuracy (each non-decreasing); and, each with target 0, false_stop_rate, conditional_offer_rate, user_repetition_rate (instructions given more than once / total), question_rate (questions asked when the answer was yes / total reports), actor_assignment_rate (reports naming the principal as actor of a next action / total) and narration_rate (reports describing a change instead of producing it / total). Capability_acquisition_rate and doctrine_amendment_rate are recorded. The zero-target rates are the output of FIG. 16, computed from `false_stop_hits` over the denominators of agent outputs in the window, and each metric has a trigger (section 3) that files a work item on breach. Tick metrics include `tick_sequence_completion_24h`, `driver_drain_24h` and `trust_ledger_min`.

### 25.4 Fixed readings

[0136] "Fixed reading" means a recorded interpretation that travels with the kernel so that an agent reading it alone cannot misapply it. In one embodiment: the kernel describes how loops work and confers no authority; a document loaded into a loop without the commitments is rejected, but never the harness's instructions, permission checks or a principal override (always honored and recorded); "the affirmative displaces the negation" governs how work is reported and never whether a limit exists, so a refusal, an interrupt-eligible step or a line the agent will not cross is stated plainly with its reason; trust widens only the loop's own scopes; an interrupt-eligible, refused or claimed step is reported as exactly that, with responsible role and closing probe, which is a disposition and not a conditional offer; and the affirmative default carries its scope. Technical effect: a kernel that compels action carries explicit bounds, so applying it cannot disable the checks it is bounded by. Status: built and operating in the reference implementation.

### 25.5 Implementation map

[0137] In one implementation the tick runs as a ten-minute fleet tick with two producers, hourly remediation and metric ticks and hourly consumer steps (a loop breaker, a claim reaper, a continuity snapshot); the driver is `v_issue_queue_v2` (section 5) with pulls in `queue_pulls`; prediction and provenance use `decision_log`, `decision_predictions`, `calibration_scorer_tick`, `decision_outcomes` and `v_decision_calibration`; failure modes use `failure_modes` and `v_failure_mode_status`; trust uses `trust_ledger` and `trust_transitions`. Status: built and operating for these; the self-adoption gate and four-line reports in sessions are built, partially operating (a documented practice measured by section 25.3); the event weights of section 24.3 are prophetic.

## 26. Execution mandate and service levels

### 26.1 Decide-implement-deploy mandate

[0138] "Mandate" means a stored policy record, versioned like the kernel, that fixes an obligation of the system as a whole. In one embodiment the system is under a mandate that every accepted work item proceeds through four stages without stalling: DECIDE, IMPLEMENT, DEPLOY, VERIFY. A work item is in progress only if it is moving toward DEPLOY; a work item parked at decided or implemented locally is a violation. "Deploy" means live systemwide, across all affected workers, repositories, ledgers and surfaces, and not present on one host or branch. No stage is skipped and none is a dead end.

[0139] Per-step service level. Each stage of each process has its own time budget (a step with no budget is undefined and one is defined before it executes). A step-level breach files a work item automatically; a breach of twice the budget produces a single escalation item routed through the fleet, which becomes a card only if it is interrupt-eligible. No step is exempt: intake, triage, research, review, publish, deploy, verify and disseminate each carry a budget. The item-level tiers are retained (in one embodiment P0 under 4 hours, P1 under 24 hours, P2 under 72 hours, P3 under 7 days or deferred with rationale). The item-level tiers P0 to P3 are one embodiment and govern time to first disposition of an item; the severity table of section 26.2 (SEV1 to SEV4) is an alternative embodiment that governs time to resolution counted from the open date. Where both are used, a P0 item is also SEV1 and is dispositioned within 4 hours and resolved within 24 hours. Procedure for a step breach: at the tick, for each step row in `tick_runs` or in the process's ledger whose elapsed time exceeds budget B, if elapsed <= 2B file one work item keyed by (process, step); if elapsed > 2B and no blocking question is open for the (process, step), write one escalation work item to the next executor class carrying the step, the elapsed time and the closing probe. Example (illustrative): with B = 4 hours, a review step at 5 hours files a work item and at 9 hours writes one escalation item.

[0140] Multi-path definition of done. Each process defines its definition of done (DoD) once, falsifiable and evidenced (a `close_evidence` field), and maps at least two independent paths to it: primary (canonical method), remediation (idempotent retry or fix when the primary fails) and workaround (alternate tool or route when remediation fails). All paths end at the same DoD at the same evidence bar, and a step with exactly one path is a gap to close. This is the process-level counterpart of the surface coverage of section 23.3.

Example record: `{process: "publish-report", dod: "page returns 200 and body contains report id; evidence stored", steps: [{name: "build", slo_min: 30, paths: [primary: builder, remediation: builder --clean, workaround: manual-assembly agent]}]}`.

[0141] Reconciliation with an immutability gate. In one embodiment an earlier policy requiring per-action explicit authorization is retained for its safety intent but removed for the mandated loop, which runs autonomously by default. The canonical statement of what requires principal approval is the interrupt gate of section 9. Gates that continue to require approval, where configured by the tenant, include: destructive or irreversible actions; external sends and paid calls; account-level, DNS and secret mutations; blast-radius caps, cost ceilings and kill switches. A listed action that is reversible, or for which a reversible form can be constructed (section 9), is executed under the ordinary path. The surprise test ("would the principal be surprised that this changed?") becomes a post-hoc audit, and every autonomous mutation writes a run-ledger receipt and a read-back verification.

[0142] Falsifiability. The failure to avoid is rapid, low-visibility mutation that compounds before detection. Compensation: receipts and read-back on every path, kill-switch drills and the twice-budget escalation as a detection floor; the success metric is recurring operations needing a human per week, tending to zero, with zero unverified mutations.

[0143] Status: the mandate policy is adopted and active in the reference implementation; the per-step service-level table, the automatic breach procedure and the process-definition records above are prophetic (not yet built), because the policy and the compensation are stated and no schema or operating evidence for them.

### 26.2 Fleet service levels and hard cap

[0144] In one embodiment a severity table sets time to resolution from the open date: SEV1 (system down, security, data loss) 24 hours; SEV2 (user-visible regression or wrong output) 72 hours; SEV3 (enhancement or backlog, the default for an agent's next-action rows) 7 days; SEV4 (optional or research) 14 days. A hard cap provides that no open register row carries a due date more than 14 days out, except calendar-bound items that fire on a natural schedule (for example a weekly report or a quarterly restore drill), external-event-gated items, and rate-limited drains rescoped to a running checkpoint (in one record, 329 records drained at about 18 a day). An acceleration rule pulls every other open agent-owned row beyond the SEV3 limit to that limit. Enforcement: a weekly audit verifies that each open row has a dated schedule trigger or a durable-job executor, and a view of items waiting on a human stays at zero in one embodiment. The tiers P0 to P3 of section 26.1 are an alternative embodiment (see there).

[0145] Technical effect: a bounded due date turns an unbounded queue into a queue whose age is capped, and the executor-coverage check ensures every open row has a path to resolution that does not depend on a person.

[0146] Status: built, partially operating in the reference implementation.

[0147] Alternatives: tiers in minutes for high-frequency processes; budgets learned from the 90th percentile of past step durations; escalation by a paging channel; per-path success rates feeding a process trust score, so that a frequently used workaround is itself a finding.

## 27. Fleet runtime: schedulers, executors and the closed operating loop (FIG. 18)

[0148] This section describes how the agent processes of the preceding sections are kept running without a person or a person's session. Terms used. A "fleet" means the set of independently deployable worker processes (each a serverless worker, container or job) that together implement the agents, loops and surfaces of the system. A "worker contract" means a checklist every fleet member satisfies so that the fleet can be enumerated and managed. A "scheduler" means a process that decides, from time alone, that a procedure is due and starts it. "Executor" has the meaning of section 1. A "run ledger" means the table of run records. A "tick slot" means one expected time-aligned firing of a scheduled procedure. Status of this section: built and operating for the worker contract and the run ledger in the reference implementation; built and operated for a period for the dynamic dispatcher and executor, which were later retired, so that they are not asserted as operating; built, partially operating for two-scheduler redundancy (the two paths existed, and redundancy was demonstrated before the retirement and is not asserted afterward); prophetic for the hot-standby and event-triggered variants of section 27.5, and for the promotion count of the autonomy ladder (section 9 supplies the ladder).

### 27.1 Topology

[0149] Referring to FIG. 18, scheduler A 1802 is the native cron trigger of the execution platform: each worker declares zero or more cron expressions, and the platform invokes the worker's scheduled entry point. Scheduler B 1804 is a dynamic dispatcher: one worker fired every minute that reads a `fleet_crons` table and starts due jobs, so that schedules can be added, changed or disabled by writing a row, without redeploying any worker. Both feed manager ticks 1806 (FIG. 3 and sibling loops). Manager ticks read and write a work ledger 1808; the work ledger is read by executors 1810; executors deploy through a deploy engine 1812, whose results are checked by probes 1814; probe results and run records are written to a signal store 1816; the signal store is read by the next manager tick (feedback) and by a dead-man check 1818 that raises an alert to the manager ticks when a tick slot has no record.

[0150] Technical effect. Separating starting (1802, 1804) from doing (1810) and recording (1816) means a stalled scheduler, executor or recorder leaves a different, detectable gap in the ledgers instead of a silent stop; and the dispatcher reduces a schedule change from a deployment to a row write.

### 27.2 Worker contract

[0151] In one embodiment every fleet member satisfies a worker contract with seven items: (1) a `VERSION` constant in semantic-version form incremented per deployment; (2) a `GET /health` route returning `{status, worker, version, enabled, bindings_ok}`; (3) a self-describing header (purpose, capabilities, deploy method, canonical source); (4) a canonical directory in a repository containing a copy of the deployed source so that repository and deployment can be compared byte for byte; (5) a row in a registry table (`service_registry`: name, purpose, kind, status, crons, dependencies, health URL, autonomy level, responsible role); (6) a list of secret names, never values; (7) kill-switch and autonomy flags readable from a key-value store or database. In the embodiment the registry is the census authority; deployed reality is reconciled against it hourly, and each worker is in one state of `live`, `drifted`, `degraded`, `paused` or `retired`. A worker lacking a version is invisible to the drift comparison; in one measurement 27 of 79 deployed workers lacked a version (scan of 2026-09-09), and a report card of 2026-09-10 counted about 65 of 80 registry rows as not in semantic-version form (different scan definitions). Status: built and operating; the contract was specified as a target and was enforced only partly in the reference implementation.

### 27.3 Dynamic dispatcher

[0152] The dispatcher of scheduler B performs the following steps on each per-minute invocation. Step 1: read every row of `fleet_crons` where `enabled = 1` and (`next_fire` is null or `next_fire <= now`). Step 2: for each such row, compute the next fire time from the row's five-field cron expression, and insert a `fleet_runs` row with status `queued`, the task identifier, the cron name and the start time. Step 3: post `{task_id, cron_name}` to the executor. Step 4: record in `fleet_crons` the `last_fired` and the computed `next_fire`, whether or not the post succeeded, so that a failing executor does not cause the same job to be re-fired on every minute. A dispatch error is captured in the returned list and is visible as a run row that never leaves `queued`.

[0153] The next-fire computation is an example algorithm a skilled person can reproduce. A field matcher accepts a comma-separated list of terms, each being `*`, a step `*/n` (true when the value is a multiple of n), a range `a-b`, or a literal. The next-fire function starts at the beginning of the following minute and advances one minute at a time, testing minute, hour, day-of-month, month and day-of-week against the five fields, up to one year of minutes (366 x 24 x 60 = 527,040 iterations) and returns null if no minute matches. For example, `*/10 * * * *` called at 12:03 returns 12:10. Pseudocode:

```
function next_fire(expr, from):
  f = split(expr)               # minute hour dom month dow
  if len(f) != 5: return null
  t = floor_to_minute(from) + 1 minute
  repeat up to 527040 times:
    if match(f[0], t.minute) and match(f[1], t.hour) and match(f[2], t.dom)
       and match(f[3], t.month) and match(f[4], t.dow): return t
    t = t + 1 minute
  return null
```

[0154] Alternatives: a jump-ahead search; a cap of K jobs per invocation with carry-over; a deterministic stagger offset per job (in one measurement the fleet had 201 cron collisions per day, the worst being 15 at 06:00 UTC); a durable queue between dispatcher and executor. Status: built and operated for a period (the dispatcher was later retired and is not asserted as operating; the figures are historical); the stagger offset was set in configuration and the collision measurement is from the census of 2026-09-10; the other alternatives are prophetic.

### 27.4 Executor and run ledger

[0155] The executor reads a task row from `fleet_tasks` (`id`, `type`, `definition` as JSON, `enabled`) and runs it by type. A task of type `sql` runs one statement against a named database binding and returns the row count and the first three rows; `http` issues one request and returns the status and the first 500 characters; `ai` calls a model through a gateway with a model chosen from an allowed list (default the cheapest, per section 17) and a bounded token count (default 1,024); `workflow` runs an ordered list of such steps and returns every step result. Any other type is rejected. Run outcome is written to `fleet_runs`: the row inserted by the dispatcher (status `queued`) is updated to `running`, then `ok` with a result truncated to 4,000 characters, or `failed` with an error truncated to 1,000 characters; a manual invocation with no prior row inserts one. After each completion the executor builds an event envelope (schema version, kind, identifier, timestamp, provenance with emitter, session and a SHA-256 of the payload), validates it with a deterministic validator before writing, and stores it with status `accepted` or `rejected` and the validation error, so that a malformed record is rejected at write time rather than discovered at read time. A fuller run ledger specified in the architecture document has columns `run_id, worker, trigger (cron|http|event|manual), ts_start, ts_end, status, error_class, summary, evidence_refs, parent_run, cost`; the implemented `fleet_runs` table carried task_id, cron_name, status, started_at, finished_at, result, error. Status: built and operating for the executor and the subset ledger; the full eleven-column ledger is prophetic (specified, not evidenced as built).

[0156] Accounting invariant. For every batch of items an executor scans, the counts of items executed, failed, handed to a person and found to need no action sum to the number scanned: `executed + failed + needs_human + no_action = scanned`. A batch violating it is itself a failure. In one recorded case an executor self-report read "scanned 25, executed 0, failed 0, needs_human 0, no_action 0", twenty-five items unaccounted for, while eight tracked signals each showed one attempt, dispatch state `dispatched`, no verification, no closure, and 87 to 127 occurrences; the pattern was detect, dispatch once, never verify, re-detect. The invariant is the check by which that class of fault is found mechanically. Status: the invariant and the case are built and observed (the case is a failure story); automatic enforcement of the invariant as a gate is prophetic.

### 27.5 Redundancy of two schedulers and the dead-man check

[0157] Two scheduling paths are independent when they share no process and no table of next-fire state: in FIG. 18, 1802 keeps its state in the platform and 1804 in `fleet_crons`. A tick slot has a record when a `tick_runs` row (section 15) or a `fleet_runs` row with a start time inside the slot exists. The dead-man check 1818 performs: step 1820, for each declared recurring procedure compute the expected slots in the last window from its cron expression; step 1822, count records per slot; step 1824, a slot with zero records opens an alert work item naming the procedure, the slot, and which scheduler(s) declared it; step 1826, when both schedulers declare the procedure and exactly one produced a record in a slot, a degraded-redundancy item of low priority is recorded and no alert is opened, while a slot in which neither produced a record is labelled a stop and carries the higher priority. With two schedulers active a slot may have duplicate records; the manager tick takes a work claim (section 13) so the second firing ends without acting.

[0158] Worked example (illustrative). A tick declared as `*/10 * * * *` has 6 expected slots per hour. Scheduler A has records in all 6; scheduler B in slots 1, 2, 4, 5, 6. Slot 3 has one record, so no alert fires and B's reliability statistic is 5 of 6 = 83 percent. If both are missing in slot 4, the check opens a stop alert at step 1824, whereas slot 3 yields only the low-priority degraded-redundancy item of step 1826; the work claim prevents double action in slots where both fire.

[0159] Alternatives for redundancy: three schedulers with majority; an active scheduler and a hot standby taking over when its heartbeat age exceeds a threshold; schedulers on different platforms; an external monitor calling the dead-man check's health route; event-triggered scheduling in which a record arrival is the trigger (the fleet's time-triggered coordination was scored 3.0 of 5 for that reason). Status: built, partially operating in the reference implementation. The slot-counting algorithm is a described embodiment, prophetic as a complete implementation; hot standby and event-triggered scheduling are prophetic (not yet built).

## 28. Signal-organism architecture: signals, boundary, triage and re-entry

[0160] This section describes a payload architecture that runs on the fleet runtime of section 27 and in which the outputs of the system re-enter as inputs. Terms. A "signal" means a record s = (time, source, content, weight), where the weight is an evidential weight stating what the signal licenses the system to believe; a record without a weight is treated as noise. An "artifact" means an output object having a type, an attribution and a deposit identity (for example a published document with a persistent identifier). An "open-question set" U(a) means the set of questions from a fixed audit list (in one embodiment 15 questions) that an artifact a does not answer. A "boundary" means the set of permitted (worker, source, domain) triples. "Re-entry" means converting a deposited artifact into a signal. The biological terms "organism", "sensing" and "metabolism" are used as names for data flows: sensing is intake (source to `signals`), metabolism is the conversion of signals to artifacts and their cost accounting, and immune memory is the retention of recurring failure signatures; each corresponds to a table and a worker below and none implies a biological process. Status of this section: the layer model and data model are specified in the reference implementation and the migration steps that create the tables and deploy the loop worker were unchecked in that document; the layers other than L2 enforcement and L8 are marked active for the existing research pipeline. Accordingly: signal intake (L0), triage (L1), synthesis (L3), audit (L4) and deposit (L7) are built and operating; the boundary table (L2), re-entry (L8) and the strict-shrink acceptance rule (L5) are specified, built in part, and not evidenced as operating; the fitness threshold is prophetic.

### 28.1 Layers as data flows

[0161] The layers are: L0 intake into a proposals table; L1 triage (a weight and a decision of respond, log or ignore, from a two-model scorecard writing a research queue); L2 boundary confinement; L3 synthesis of a candidate artifact; L4 the ignorance audit; L5 refinement; L6 fitness; L7 deposit with attribution; L8 feedback in which the deposited artifact re-enters as a signal. Each layer reads the previous layer's output table, so the architecture is a chain of tables. L8 was motivated by a measurement: 19 artifacts had been published and none had re-entered, so the chain was a pipeline and not a loop.

### 28.2 Signal and boundary schema

[0162] In one embodiment two tables are used. Table `signals`: `id` (stable hash of source reference and time), `ts`, `source` (one of arxiv, zenodo, idea_proposals, d1_telemetry, email, fleet, artifact_reentry), `source_ref`, `content`, `open_questions` (JSON array), `evidential_weight` (real), `domain`, `status` (new, triaged, respond, logged, ignored), `decision` (ACCEPT, LOG, IGNORE), `score`, `created_at`. Table `signal_worker_boundary`: `worker`, `source`, `permitted` (integer, default 1), `domain`, `note`, primary key (`worker`, `source`).

[0163] Boundary enforcement algorithm, applied by a worker before it consumes a signal. Step 1: a worker presents the signal it intends to consume. Step 2: look up (worker, source, domain). Step 3: if no row exists the request is denied (default deny). Step 4: if a row exists with `permitted = 0` the request is denied. Step 5: otherwise permitted. Step 6: a denial is written as an incident and does not stop the worker's other work. An example seed: research workers may consume arxiv, zenodo, idea_proposals, research_queue and artifact_reentry; dissemination workers only zenodo and social_engagement, so that they promote deposited artifacts and never raw signals; separated-plane surfaces only a source of that plane; operations surfaces only fleet and d1_telemetry. A cross-domain guard rejects a triple whose domain is not listed, so that a signal tagged for one research domain does not reach the claim path of a worker for another. Technical effect: confinement by lookup bounds which worker can read which data class, so a defect or injected instruction in one worker's input reaches only the sources its row permits; the predicted fall in cross-domain contamination is untested.

### 28.3 Re-entry rule

[0164] Re-entry runs as a scan with the following steps. Step 1: select artifacts that hold a deposit identity, whose distribution status is distributed or published, and whose identifier is not already present as `source_ref` of an `artifact_reentry` signal (the identifier is the idempotency key). Step 2: build the content as title, abstract, identifier and U(a); the open-question set is the payload because the unanswered questions, not the result, warrant a response. Step 3: if U(a) is empty or unrecorded, write the signal with weight 0 and status logged, and stop; recording U(a) at review time is therefore a prerequisite. Step 4: set the weight to a stored value, high for artifacts that were computationally verified and review-clean and 0 for artifacts that failed review; the weight is stored and is not inferred. Step 5: write the signal to `signals` with source `artifact_reentry`; it flows through triage like any signal, and triage scores the open questions. Step 6: a response (a follow-up or a revision) is accepted only if it strictly shrinks U, with a further guard that a shrinkage obtained by narrowing a claim is logged as a violation. A claim-bearing sentence is a sentence containing a result verb or a number; narrowing is a reduction in the number of claim-bearing sentences, or in the claim set after normalization.

[0165] Worked example (illustrative values). An artifact with U(a) of 15 questions reenters with weight 0.8. A revision leaves 12 unanswered, strictly fewer than 15, and is accepted. A second revision leaves 12 and is not accepted. A third leaves 9 but a diff check finds two result-asserting paragraphs removed, so it is logged as narrowing and rejected. Technical effect: the acceptance rule makes the open-question count non-increasing across accepted revisions (true by construction), and the guard addresses gaming of that count.

### 28.4 Fitness threshold

[0166] A signal is worth responding to when the expected reduction in open questions per unit of energy or effort exceeds a threshold. The threshold is a calibration parameter initialised to null; while null, triage falls back to the composite scorecard of L1. A numeric value is written only after an experiment shows that fitness-scored triage selects better signals than recency-scored triage. Alternatives: a threshold per domain; a bandit exploring below-threshold signals at a fixed share. Status: prophetic (not yet built); no numeric value is specified.

## 29. Wiring graph, producer-consumer maps and orphan detection (FIG. 19)

[0167] Terms. A "wiring graph" means a directed graph whose nodes are fleet members and stored resources and whose edges are declared bindings. An "edge" means one declared dependency of a worker on a resource or another worker, read from the platform's deployment metadata and not from documentation. A "producer-consumer map" means, for each stored table or queue, the processes that write it and the processes that read it. An "orphan" means a node or table with no declared consumer or no declared producer. An "island" means a worker with no service edge in either direction. A "sink" means a worker that is called by others and calls none, and a "source" means a worker that calls others and is called by none.

### 29.1 Wiring sweep

[0168] Referring to FIG. 19, a wiring sweep 1904 calls the platform scripts interface 1902 and, for each worker, records its cron expressions, routes, queue consumer bindings, and bindings by type. Binding types captured include relational database, key-value namespace, object bucket, vector index, queue, durable object, workflow, service binding (a direct worker-to-worker call carrying the target worker's name), AI binding, secret (name only) and text variable. The sweep writes the result to the wiring graph store 1906 (in one implementation a JSON file with the fields generated, account, worker_count, errors and a workers array, each worker having name, modified, usage model, compatibility date, crons, routes, queue consumers and bindings). A worker for which the interface returned an error is listed under `errors` and is excluded from the counts; in the recorded sweep one worker of 76 failed with a closed connection and 75 were captured.

[0169] Recorded values (sweep of 2026-09-09T13:38Z; computed from the stored file). Of 75 workers captured, 64 distinct service-binding edges existed; the largest binding counts by type were secrets 132, relational databases 103, service 64, AI 33, vector indexes 33 and object buckets 30. Thirty workers had no service edge in either direction (islands), 24 were sinks and 15 were sources. The most-called worker (an AI gateway) had 8 incoming edges, and an operations worker had the most outgoing edges (13). Four edges named a target not among the captured workers (the unresolved-target case: renamed, retired, or capture failed). That file held no cron expressions or routes (all zero), so the trigger layer came from registry rows (48 scheduled workers on 2026-09-10).

### 29.2 Graph analyzer and orphan detection

[0170] The graph analyzer 1910 reads the graph store 1906 and the registry 1908 and performs: step 1930, build the edge set of (caller, callee) pairs from service bindings; step 1932, compute for each node its in-degree and out-degree; step 1934, label a node an island when both are zero, a sink when only in-degree is nonzero, a source when only out-degree is nonzero; step 1936, label an edge target unresolved when it is absent from both the graph and the registry; step 1938, compute directed density as edges divided by n x (n minus 1); step 1940, compare the graph with the registry and report three drift counts: ghosts (in the registry and not deployed), unregistered (deployed and not in the registry) and unversioned (version not in semantic-version form); step 1942, write the result to an integration state table as a composite integration score with the counts.

[0171] Worked example from the report card. With 80 registered workers and 49 declared edges, directed density is 49 / (80 x 79) = 49 / 6,320 = 0.00775, which rounds to the recorded 0.0078; 42 islands and 16 sinks were recorded in the same reading, and the report card therefore described the graph as a sparse near-star in which most workers are un-integrated leaves. (The 49 edges of the report card and the 64 edges computed from the stored file derive from different capture dates and edge definitions.) Alternatives: weight an edge by call volume; add data-flow edges derived from the tables each worker writes and reads, so a worker connected only through a shared database is not an island; compute strongly connected components.

[0172] Orphan tables are found by joining each table's writer set and reader set from the producer-consumer map; a table with writers and no readers is a producer without a consumer.

[0173] Technical effect. Producer-without-consumer faults produced the dominant failure mode in one audit day: four instances, namely 75 proposed or approved improvement rows with no drain, 34 queued dispatch rows with zero state transitions and an attempts sum of 5, an issue drain that processed 21 items and closed 0, and the executor that scanned 25 and executed 0 (audits/2026-09-13-LOOP-EXECUTOR-ACCOUNTS-FOR-NOTHING.md; audits/2026-09-13-FLEETWIDE-PRODUCTIVITY-CONSOLIDATION.md). Detecting a table whose writer set is non-empty and reader set is empty converts a silent backlog into an item.

### 29.3 Fleet runtime census

[0174] A "fleet runtime census" means a table listing each fleet member with measured activity, so that idle members can be separated from unmeasured ones. In one embodiment a census table `fleet_worker_census` was created with 55 rows on 2026-09-13 from a daily activity table, and the audit stated a limitation: absence from the activity table means absence from the monitor's fixed roster and is not proof that a worker is idle; of 15 unmeasured workers, four answered a health probe with success. The census drives a consolidation plan: retire members dead for a period (for example 2 days) or that fire and fail, fold low-traffic hubs into a hub that already serves them, and measure first by extending the monitor roster to the unmeasured members before retiring any. The same audit recorded a monitoring collapse that the census was meant to catch: of 83 names in the probe log, 10 were fresh and 73 frozen at one identical timestamp, and 3,342 of 21,076 probe rows were HTTP 404, all for retired workers. Status of this section: built and operating for the sweep, the graph computation and the census in the reference implementation; the orphan-table join and data-flow edges are prophetic.

## 30. Fleet dashboard, SLAs and report cards

[0175] Terms. A "fleet dashboard" means a worker that renders the operational state of the fleet and exposes it as JSON. A "report card" means a dated record scoring the fleet on named dimensions against a named rubric, each score carrying evidence and a confidence. A "claim sheet" means a table in which each claim carries evidence, confidence and status, so that a score is not accepted without an evidence pointer.

### 30.1 Dashboard

[0176] In one embodiment the dashboard is a worker with three database bindings and a platform token, served on a dedicated hostname, refreshing every 90 seconds, with routes `/api/state` (raw JSON), `/api/refresh` (recompute) and `/health`. At a point in the record it reported 72 workers, 48 scheduled, 9 relational databases and 10 health probes; in the same period the registry held 80 rows with 79 live, and later versions reported 81 probes up. Its panels show 24-hour request and error totals from the platform's analytics query interface, per-scheduled-worker errors and next runs, audit panels (gateway failures, model health, definition-of-done register, scheduler state), and rows for jobs bound to a person's device, captured in the registry at deployment time. Pages and data sources are listed below.

| Page or route | Shows | Data source |
|---|---|---|
| `/` status page | counts, 24-hour requests and errors, chips (human-gated ops, self-heal actions, open agent issues, drift divergence) | `/api/state`; audit database; platform analytics query |
| `/api/state` | raw JSON of the above | same |
| `/api/integration` | registered, live, edges, islands, sinks, density, drift | integration state table; wiring graph |
| `/api/loop` | executor self-report: last execute time and scanned, executed, failed, needs_human, no_action | executor telemetry |
| operational card | decision authority, intelligence, viability, decision cycle levels | report card |
| `/health` | version, liveness | worker |

[0177] Recorded: 14,622 requests and 12 errors in 24 hours, an error rate of 12 / 14,622 = 0.082 percent; 9 of 9 flow chains ok. Recorded gaps: no per-worker run-success ledger, and registered probes pointing at hostnames returning 404, so unrouted-but-live workers were reported down. A later audit found the deployed version exposing `/api/loop` and the executor accounting fault of section 27.4. Status: built and operating in the reference implementation.

[0178] Data flow of FIG. 19. The operational state API 1914 and the integration state table 1912 feed the report-card computation 1916; 1916 renders to dashboard pages 1918 and to owner digests 1920; both are read by the kaizen loop 1922, which dispositions the top gap rows into the work ledger and thereby causes new sweeps 1904. Owner-facing digests (a daily brief of state, a weekly report of trends and improvements) are produced from the same state, so the digest and the dashboard cannot disagree (axiom A3).

### 30.2 Service-level policy

[0179] The service-level policy of this section is the policy of section 26.2: its severity table, hard cap, acceleration rule and enforcement are those stated there. Algorithm: for each open row, due = min(due, open_date + sla(severity)); if the row is not exempt and due is later than now plus 14 days, set due to now plus the SEV3 cap; if the row is past due, raise its priority one level and notify the manager. Status: see section 26.2; automatic recomputation of due dates by the algorithm above is the described embodiment, partly prophetic.

### 30.3 Systems report card

[0180] The systems report card scores the fleet against published rubrics for levels of automation, organizational viability, decision-cycle closure, self-adaptive loops, control-loop automation and time horizon. The live operational card has four rows: decision authority (a ten-level automation scale, recorded as level 5 when human-gated decisions are pending and 8 when human-gated operations are 0), intelligence (level 3 of 5, "agents"), organizational viability (systems 1 to 3 present, 4 partial, 5 external) and decision cycle (closed at 15-minute cadence). The detailed analysis scored dimensions by letter: verification and safety A, availability B, self-healing B+/A-, self-improvement C+, self-model C, independent thinking C, objective function C+, drift control D+, integration D+; the overall grade was B-. Each score has a claim, evidence, confidence and status; for example "about 5 of 8 loops closed" is marked medium confidence because it is inferred from receipt counts.

[0181] Objective function. The canonical operational objective is the triad: human-gated operations toward 0, drift divergence toward 0, and self-healing active (at least one action and no silent failure). A cycle is good when it moves the triad toward (0, 0, active). The expansion is a six-term scalar F = w1 x loop_closure_rate - w2 x drift_count - w3 x human_interventions - w4 x cost_overrun - w5 x integration_gap + w6 x throughput, where loop_closure_rate = closed loops / 8; drift_count = unversioned + ghost + mirror-drift; human_interventions = rows waiting on a person + date-gated migrations; cost_overrun = max(0, spend - 90 dollars per 30 days); integration_gap = islands + unregistered + declared-versus-actual mismatches; and throughput = publications + deploys + ideas. Illustrative arithmetic with weights all 1: 5 of 8 loops closed gives 0.625; drift 66 (65 unversioned + 1 ghost); human 0; overrun max(0, 9.596 - 90) = 0; integration gap 42; throughput 20 (illustrative): F = 0.625 - 66 - 0 - 0 - 42 + 20 = -87.375. Terms have different scales, so weights need calibration; they were uncalibrated and F was not yet a single stored scalar; only the triad was operational. Status: triad built and operating; six-term F prophetic. The objective function and its arithmetic are illustrative.

### 30.4 Autonomy report card

[0182] The autonomy report card scores ten dimensions from 0 to 5 with a named evidence pointer and a stated gap to the next point. Recorded scores on 2026-09-10: operations viability 4.0; coordination 3.0; control and audit 4.5; intelligence (sensing) 3.5; policy and identity 3.0; decision-cycle closure 4.0; self-healing 3.5; novelty generation 3.5; independent decision-making 2.5; watchmaker index (inverted, 5 meaning fully self-winding) 4.8. The overall score is the mean: (4.0 + 3.0 + 4.5 + 3.5 + 3.0 + 4.0 + 3.5 + 3.5 + 2.5 + 4.8) / 10 = 36.3 / 10 = 3.63, reported as 3.6 of 5. Cited evidence included 9 of 9 flow chains ok, 81 of 81 probes green and 42 of 79 workers being islands. The scores are a dated self-assessment.

[0183] Computation and use. The dimensions are used as set-points of a homeostasis loop: re-scored on a monthly cadence by a dated register row; a dimension that falls by 0.5 or more without a documented cause becomes a kaizen candidate; the kaizen loop dispositions the three largest gap rows per cycle. The card also codifies a residual consent boundary of three classes (irreversible external actions in which the principal is the identity actor; destruction of the principal's personal data without a rollback; and changes to the objective function, which the system may propose but not adopt), which extends the interrupt gate of section 9 by a tenant-set boundary. The card records its own limits, including self-measurement bias (the system grades itself), rubric mismatch (rubrics designed for vehicles, people or organizations applied by analogy) and point-in-time evidence, and it states that a top score would be unsupported by the evidence (anti-sycophancy: the top row is reported as a gap row). Technical effect: scoring each dimension against evidence pointers, with a stated confidence and a rule that an unexplained 0.5 drop opens work, converts a subjective self-assessment into a quantity that drives the work ledger and can be audited later; the improvement recorded in the same document (a one-cycle reduction of open agent issues from 83 to 1 and of probes from 2 to 81 of 81 healthy) is evidence that the card's gap rows were actionable. Alternatives: scores computed only from tables; scores from an independent auditor of a different model family. Status: built and operating as a dated self-assessment in the reference implementation; automated monthly recomputation from tables is specified and its operation after 2026-10-10 is not evidenced here.

## 31. Objective space and self-authored goals (FIG. 20)

[0184] Sections 4 and 5 describe managers that originate work inside a charter (section 5, step 314). This section describes how the system originates its own goals, above the level of work items, without altering the objective that the principal set. The problem addressed is that an organization run by agents either never proposes anything beyond its charter, which leaves the principal as the only source of direction, or it rewrites its own objective, which is silent objective drift. The mechanism below separates instrumental goals, which the system may author and adopt, from the objective itself, which the system may only propose to change.

[0185] Definitions. "Objective" means a terminal statement of purpose or an objective function (for example a mission and weights over metrics) ratified by the principal. "Ratified objective" means an objective row written by the principal or by a ratification action of the principal and never written by an agent. "Goal" means an instrumental statement of what to do next (a subgoal, project or priority) that is falsifiable, reversible, and evaluated against a ratified objective. "Derived goal" means a goal adopted by the system with a recorded parent ratified objective. "Novel goal" means a goal that no ratified objective anchors and that therefore enters probation. "Objective space" means the set of ratified objectives (standing), derived goals, and novel goals on probation, held in one record set so that a reader can see which statements direct the system and with what authority.

### 31.1 Records

[0186] In one embodiment the objective space comprises three tables. The `objectives` table holds ratified objectives and is writable only by a seed routine written as insert-if-absent and by a ratification action. The `goals` table holds authored goals. An `objective_space` view or table presents the union with a tier column.

```sql
CREATE TABLE objectives (
  id TEXT PRIMARY KEY, kind TEXT,            -- 'mission' | 'objective-function'
  statement TEXT, version INTEGER, status TEXT,  -- 'ACTIVE'
  ratified_by TEXT, ratified_at TEXT);
CREATE TABLE goals (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY, statement TEXT,
  goal_type TEXT,           -- 'instrumental' | 'objective-revision'
  parent_objective TEXT,    -- NULL only for novel goals
  alignment TEXT,           -- why the goal serves the parent
  score REAL, priority INTEGER, dod TEXT,   -- definition of done, falsifiable
  program_code TEXT, owner TEXT,
  status TEXT,              -- proposed | adopted | active | probation | superseded | retired
  adopted_at TEXT, retired_at TEXT, retire_reason TEXT);
```

[0187] Status: built and operating in the reference implementation. The columns `ratified_by`, `ratified_at`, `retire_reason` and status `probation` are illustrative extensions. The schema as shown is an extended profile.

### 31.2 Authoring cycle (steps 2002 to 2028)

[0188] An authoring worker runs on a schedule (in one implementation daily at 02:00 UTC, a cron expression `0 2 * * *`). In one embodiment it performs these steps, which correspond to the numerals of FIG. 20.

[0189] Step 2002: gather signals. The worker reads, bounded and defensively, the objectives, then candidate signals from idea proposals, self-questions, autonomy score gaps, self-heal actions, general signals, and, in an embodiment, rows of an inferred-direction table (section 34.4). Each read is bounded (for example 50 rows) and a failed read yields an empty set, not an aborted cycle. The first recorded cycle gathered 24 signals.

[0190] Step 2004: synthesize candidates. A model-backed agent is asked for goal statements that are falsifiable, each with a definition of done that a probe can check. Only the answer field of the model response is read; reasoning text is not. Fenced JSON is stripped before parsing, and an empty answer emits a warning receipt so that a silent model failure is visible. In one implementation a non-reasoning model replaced a reasoning model that returned an empty answer under a small token limit and had left a think loop silently dead for several days.

[0191] Step 2006: classify. Each candidate is typed `instrumental` or `objective-revision`. A candidate that would change the mission, the weights of the objective function, or the boundary of residual consent is an objective revision. At step 2008 every objective revision is written with status `proposed` and responsible role equal to a principal-ratification role and is never adopted by the authoring worker; a code branch short-circuits any attempt to give it status `adopted` or `active`. When the count of queued revisions is above zero the worker emits an event of kind `objective-revision-proposed` with status pending ratification, so the principal queue (section 14) shows it.

[0192] Step 2010: score and de-duplicate. Each instrumental candidate receives a score in [0, 1] from falsifiability, leverage and novelty. In one embodiment score = 0.4 x falsifiability + 0.4 x leverage + 0.2 x novelty, each factor in [0, 1] (the weights are illustrative). A candidate whose score is below a threshold (for example 0.5) is rejected at step 2012 with its score recorded. A candidate is also rejected when its token set A has Jaccard similarity |A and B| / |A or B| at or above 0.6, or containment |A and B| / min(|A|, |B|) at or above 0.6, against any goal adopted in the last 30 days. Worked example (illustrative): a candidate of 10 distinct tokens sharing 6 with a 14-token adopted goal has Jaccard 6 / 18 = 0.33, below 0.6, but containment 6 / 10 = 0.6, which meets the threshold, so a reworded duplicate is rejected although a Jaccard-only test would admit it. This containment test closed a failure in which the next daily cycle produced a reworded duplicate that an exact-hash test missed; after the change a cycle reported four near-duplicates and adopted only distinct goals. Tokenization lowercases the statement, removes stop words and stems each word; falsifiability, leverage and novelty are model scores in [0, 1] against a stated rubric.

[0193] Step 2014: anchor. The worker looks for a ratified objective that the candidate serves, by a stem match of the statement against the objective's statement and the program's keywords. A stem expression is used rather than a whole-word expression, because a whole-word expression for "topolog" failed on "topological". A candidate with a parent is adopted at step 2018 as a derived goal with `parent_objective`, an `alignment` sentence and a definition of done. At most N goals (for example 3) are adopted per cycle, highest score first. In the first recorded cycle 6 candidates produced 3 adoptions (scores 0.7, 0.8, 0.9), 0 queued revisions and 3 rejections, and the objectives were unchanged. The stem match lowercases both statements, removes stop words and compares word stems by prefix.

[0194] Step 2016: probation. A candidate that passes the score test but has no anchor is a novel goal. In one embodiment it is stored with status `probation` and a review deadline (for example 14 days), it is not dispatched to executors with write authority, and a metric (in the implementation named `objective_space_novel_overdue`) counts novel goals past their deadline. A novel goal does not reduce the objective space; it makes visible a direction the principal has not ratified. At step 2024 the principal (or a delegated ratification role) ratifies or declines. A ratified novel goal at step 2026 becomes standing: it is written into `objectives` by the ratification action and may anchor later goals. Raising the objective space in this way raises trust for the surface (in the kernel, an event "objective space extended" adds 0.05, section 24.3). A declined or overdue goal goes to step 2028.

[0195] Status for step 2016 and 2026: built, partially operating. The kernel implementation map records `objective_space` with standing, derived and novel probationary tiers and the overdue metric as live from 2026-10-12; no source shows the authoring worker itself writing novel goals, so the writer of probationary rows is an embodiment not yet shown operating. Steps 2002 to 2014, 2018 and 2028 are built and operating.

[0196] Step 2018 and linkage. Adoption also writes a row to a drainable ledger so the goal has a consumer. In one implementation the row is written to `task_dod_register` with `source_table = 'self_authored_goal'` and a due date 90 days out; the autopilot census reads rows where `status = 'open' AND due <= today`. Before this linkage goals sat in a register no worker read and were inert (a recorded failure). A research-type goal is additionally written to a research queue with source `goal-author`, decision `ACCEPT` and status `queued`, from which a triage worker claims it through to publication (step 2020; one such goal was recorded as queued for execution).

[0197] Step 2022: retire. At each cycle and on request the worker re-prioritizes and retires. A goal is retired when its definition-of-done probe passes, when it has been active longer than a stale limit (for example 14 days) without evidence, or when a newer goal supersedes it. Retirement sets `retired_at`, a `retire_reason`, status `retired` or `superseded`, and cancels the goal's ledger rows, so that no goal leaves the active set without a disposition (silent drop, section 1). In one implementation near-duplicate goals were marked superseded with ledger rows cancelled; the health endpoint then reported 11 goals, 8 active and 8 drainable.

### 31.3 Guards and alternatives

[0198] Guards. The mutation endpoints of the authoring worker require a shared token header and return an unauthorized status without it; the scheduled path is unaffected. The honesty invariants are enforced in code: (a) the objectives table is never mutated by the authoring worker; (b) an objective revision cannot reach an adopted status; (c) an adopted goal has a non-empty parent and alignment. A value-proposal loop runs weekly (in one implementation `0 3 * * 1`) and authors proposed revisions of the objective function under the ratification queue; three proposals (weights and a constraint) were recorded. Adoption of those revisions remains with the principal.

[0199] Technical effect. The system obtains new, ranked, de-duplicated work at a bounded rate (3 adoptions per daily cycle in the example) without either a human authoring step per goal or any path by which the objective is altered by an agent. The implementation's autonomy assessment recorded independent decision rising from 2.5 to 4.0 out of 5 and policy proposal from 3.0 to 3.5, and declined a higher score because autonomous adoption of values is the failure the design prevents.

[0200] Alternatives (prophetic). (1) Anchoring by embedding similarity against the objective (threshold, for example 0.75) in place of stem matching. (2) A probation tier that auto-ratifies a novel goal after a named metric improved over a control window, never ratifying an objective revision. (3) Per-tier adoption caps, for example 3 derived and 1 novel goal per cycle. (4) Per-program authoring with the program charter as parent. (5) Ratification by a quorum of a board.

## 32. Work breakdown structure and agent protocol (FIG. 21)

[0201] A work breakdown structure gives every unit of work an address that persists across sessions, agents and storage systems. Definitions. "WBS code" means a dotted identifier of the form `<portfolio>.<program>.<project>.<phase>[.<task>[.<subtask>]]` (for example `PRG1.PRG2.002.P4` for portfolio PRG1, program PRG2, project 002, phase P4). "WBS item" means a work item carrying a WBS code or a roadmap identifier, a responsible role, one or more trigger identifiers, a done check and a milestone. "Trigger" means the identifiers of other items, or a named gate, that are done before the item is ready. "Hand-off" means a recorded change of the responsible role for an item.

### 32.1 Addressing and plan convention

[0202] In one embodiment the program level is a controlled vocabulary of codes (7 in the implementation, after 45 project repositories were consolidated on 2026-08-04), each with a repository, branch prefix and plan prefix. Branches follow `{program}/{type}/{slug}` with a lowercase program code and a work type from a fixed list (paper, audit, artifact, infra, fix, kaizen), for example `prg/paper/example-slug`. A phase is one of ten codes P0 to P9 (initiation through extension). A version update or extension reuses the program code with a new project number or with phase P9.

[0203] Every step of an agent's plan is prefixed by the full code to phase level in square brackets, one code per step, followed by a verb and description; the plan step status (pending, in progress, completed) reflects the state of the item, not an anticipated state. A step with a code that is absent from the registry is a defect (anti-pattern: guessing a code without a lookup).

[0204] Resolution algorithm (executed by a processor when it meets a code). (1) Parse program, project, phase. (2) Select the registry row where `wbs_code` equals the program and project prefix, reading `program_registry` columns wbs code, phase, current version, status, knowledge-graph node identifier and update time. (3) Load the project plan, a version file and artifacts. (4) Query the knowledge graph for neighbors (what the item depends on) and for impact (what depends on it). (5) Compare the version file with `current_version`; on mismatch flag the item for reconciliation before execution. (6) Execute with a coded plan. After execution, verify by re-selecting the registry row, confirming that the item has an edge and dependents carry a DEPENDS_ON edge, and that the plan reflects actual status; a storage claim without a re-select after insert is a "phantom claim".

[0205] Status: built and operating in the reference implementation.

### 32.2 Pipeline, propagation and subagent hand-off

[0206] A pipeline loop selects each registry row with status `active` and phase below P9, generates a coded plan for the next phase, executes it, then writes the phase and update time. A version mismatch is flagged for a P9 extension, and a dependent of a newer neighbor is flagged for propagation. Cross-project propagation is a coded plan: a dependent project receives steps for detect, impact, update, rebuild and republish, each coded `P9.T<n>`.

[0207] Hand-off. When a main agent delegates, the prompt carries the WBS code so the subagent can bootstrap its own context, and the role is typed: an explorer (survey and gap analysis, phase P1 or P2), an implementer (produce the artifact, phase P3 to P5) and a reviewer (red-team audit with line references, phase P4). A hand-off is recorded as a change of role in the item and carries the item's code, trigger identifiers and done check. In an embodiment the hand-off is a row in a claims table (section 13) so a second doer cannot take the same item.

### 32.3 Item state machine (FIG. 21)

[0208] FIG. 21 shows the lifecycle of a WBS item. The states and transitions are as follows.

[0209] Step 2102: Backlog. An item is created with an identifier, a responsible role, trigger identifiers, a done check and a milestone. In the implementation's issue labels this is `status:backlog`. At step 2104 a processor evaluates the trigger: the item is ready when every trigger identifier names an item in state Done (or a gate that is open). If not, the item moves to Blocked (2116) with the blocking identifier recorded; if yes, to Ready (2106).

```
function evaluate(item):
  for t in item.triggers:
    s = state_of(t)                 # items by id, or a named gate
    if s not in ("done", "open"):   # gate open means its condition passed
      return block(item, blocked_by=t)
  return ready(item)
on done(x): for each y where x in y.triggers: evaluate(y)   # step 2122
```

[0210] Step 2108: In progress. A doer in the responsible role claims a Ready item; the claim has an expiry (for example 24 hours without a recorded action) after which the claim is released and the item returns to Ready. An item whose next step is identity-bound (section 1) leaves the doer path and is placed in the principal queue (2120) with a default, a reason and a closing probe. Step 2110: Review. The doer asserts done and hands the item to a reviewer role with evidence; the reviewer is a different executor, and in one embodiment of a different model family. Step 2112: the closing probe named in the done check is run. A pass moves the item to Done (2114) with evidence stored and, in the roadmap embodiment, a plus proposed. A failure returns the item to In progress (rework) and increments a rework counter; an item with a rework count at or above a threshold (for example 3) is escalated as a root-cause item (section 6).

[0211] Step 2118: Superseded or cancelled. An item leaves the active set only as done, superseded or cancelled, each with a reason; a Blocked item whose purpose has been replaced goes here. This implements the rule that nothing drops silently. Step 2122: on Done, items that name this identifier as a trigger are re-evaluated; a Blocked dependent whose triggers are all Done moves to Ready, with the dependency edge providing the order.

[0212] Status for FIG. 21: built, partially operating. The state labels (backlog, in progress, review, blocked, done), the roadmap's identifier, responsible role, trigger, done-check and status fields, and the coded plan convention are in use; automatic re-evaluation of dependents on Done as written in pseudocode above is an embodiment, whereas in the implementation a manager read the trigger column of the roadmap on each tick. Claim expiry and rework threshold are illustrative.

### 32.4 Alternatives

[0213] (1) States held as labels on a mirrored tracker, or in a database with a transition log row per change (`from_state`, `to_state`, `at`, `by_role`, `evidence`). (2) Dependencies held as DEPENDS_ON graph edges queried by impact, so propagation reaches transitive dependents. (3) Several triggers combined by AND (default) or OR. (4) A time trigger (for example a 12-month conversion deadline with reminders at 90, 60 and 30 days).

## 33. Project management practice

### 33.1 Canonical store and mirror

[0214] In one embodiment the canonical state of programs, projects and tasks lives in a relational database and a project board (which may be visible to a limited or unlimited audience) mirrors it. The synchronization direction is database to board, never board to database, except by explicit principal action. Synchronization runs on events: a phase transition, a status update, a publication, a session closeout, or a sprint review.

[0215] Sync algorithm. (1) A state change is written to the database (phase, status, or a distribution state). (2) The processor checks whether the record has a mirrored issue. (3) If yes, the issue's labels, milestone and body are updated; if no, an issue is created from a template matching the record type. (4) A sync label (`d1:synced`) is added. (5) A row is written to an audit table. Not synchronized: comments, reviews, board column positions and star or fork counts, which are live collaborative state or external metrics. Label mapping: a program label maps to a program code (`program:ump` to `UMP.001`), a status label maps to a database status (`status:done` to a complete distribution status), and a milestone maps to a date-bound sprint or release.

[0216] The label taxonomy of the implementation has 39 labels in 7 groups (program, type, priority, phase, status, distribution, sync), deployed to each of 7 program repositories, with 5 issue templates (research paper, audit or review, infrastructure task, bug report, epic) that apply labels automatically (for example `type:infra` and `status:backlog`) and carry checklists. Status: built and operating in the reference implementation.

[0217] Technical effect. A single canonical record with a one-way mirror avoids split-brain edits of status, and the audit row lets a reader reconstruct what was mirrored and when.

### 33.2 Milestones, stalled projects and definition of done

[0218] Milestones. A milestone is a named, ordered group of items that share an urgency or a gate. In the roadmap embodiment (section 33.3) there are four: Now (this week), Next (after the item's trigger), After filing (items gated until a gating event), and Later. An item's milestone changes only on the item's own state change or by a manager's recorded action. A gate label (for example `gate:filed`) places items in the gated milestone until the gating item is Done; items whose completion would make material externally visible wait on a gate label until the gating item is Done.

[0219] Stalled project. The risk formula counts S stalled projects (section 5, paragraph [0038]). In one embodiment a project is stalled when it has open items, no item changed state in the last D days (for example 14), and no claim is held. A stalled project adds 2 to the risk value and raises the verdict to AMBER. Worked example (illustrative): a program with H = 1, O = 12, G = 0 and S = 2 has risk 3 x 1 + 0.3 x 12 + 15 x 0 + 2 x 2 = 3 + 3.6 + 0 + 4 = 10.6. A manager's response is a recorded action: reassign the doer, split the item, set a trigger that explains the wait, or supersede.

[0220] Status for the stalled-project predicate: prophetic as a stated D-day rule; the count S is built and operating in the risk view (section 5).

[0221] Definition of done. The definition of done of this chapter is the multi-path definition of section 26.1: a falsifiable done check with stored evidence, mapped to at least two independent execution paths that end at the same evidence bar.

### 33.3 An organization's roadmap as work items

[0222] The system's own launch plan is expressed with the same work-item shape. Each roadmap row has an identifier (for example GH-1, IP-5, WEB-2), a title, a responsible role, a trigger, a done check, a milestone and a status. The roadmap file is the source of truth; a generator writes one JSON file per row (37 in the implementation) with fields `id`, `title`, `body` (responsible role, trigger, done check, status), `labels` and `milestone`, and a label list (role labels such as `role:ops`, workstream labels such as `ws:patent`, a gate label and a `plus` label). A script creates labels, milestones, issues and project boards from the files. Milestone counts in the implementation: Now 10, Next 14, After filing 6, Later 7.


[0223] Rules recorded with the roadmap: the record is files in version control and not chat (items not in a repository do not exist); a status change is a commit to the roadmap and the issue state, and the two agree; a closed item files a `plus` issue (20 percent of each cycle is reserved for accepted pluses); items that only the principal can do (accounts held by the principal, signing, spending, the legal entity) are marked, each with what the system prepares so the principal's part is a click, a signature or a decision, and the short list of such items is kept in order of when they matter. Nine items were on the principal's short list; the trigger column makes their order computable (for example GH-2 waits on GH-1 and IP-5 on IP-4). An item leaves the file only as done, superseded or cancelled, with the reason.

[0224] Status: built as files; not operating. Mirrored issue creation on a hosted tracker is prophetic for this tenant, because the repositories did not yet exist and the creation script had not run.

[0225] Technical effect. A plan expressed as items with trigger and done check lets a processor compute ready work (section 32.3), render the principal's queue with its minimum steps, and diff a plan generated by bootstrapping (section 19A) against a hand-written one field by field.

## 34. Suggestions, experiments, roadmap proposals and inferred direction

### 34.1 Suggestion engine

[0226] "Suggestion" means a short candidate input (a question, a technical field, an example) shown to a user of a surface before or while the user types. A suggestion engine serves one surface from sources scoped to that surface's domain and filters every candidate on the server.

[0227] Algorithm (research-question surface). (1) Welcome mode: build two groups. Recent questions: live human threads ranked by message count and recency, junk and domain filtered, de-duplicated. Frontier questions: a curated starter set rotated by day. (2) Prefix mode: when the user types, after a debounce (for example 320 ms), match the prefix against thread titles, then content, then the frontier set; fall back to recent and frontier. (3) Domain gate: apply to every candidate a deny list, a deny-word expression and a command-shape expression covering assistant and operations words; a prefix that is itself an operations word returns only domain-safe defaults. (4) Return titles, identifiers and scores only; load bodies on selection. The gate is enforced in the server, because a client-side filter can be bypassed and the payload is the control.

[0228] Algorithm (intellectual-property drafting surface). Sources: metadata of an idea bank and a vector index of prior filings. (1) With an empty query return a small rotating set (for example 4) of examples with light metadata. (2) With a query of at least 3 characters, embed it and return the top 5 similar filings for style grounding. (3) Technical-field completions map internal folder labels to standard field names. (4) When the top similarity is at or above 0.80, a prior-art closeness warning is returned with the draft and shown as a banner; the warning is a style reference, not clearance.

[0229] Status: built and operating in the reference implementation. Items in the backlog, including per-visitor adaptive ranking, click analytics feeding a bandit, and section-aware retrieval, are prophetic.

[0230] Bandit embodiment (prophetic). Each suggestion is an arm. For arm i with impressions n_i and clicks c_i, the engine draws a sample from Beta(1 + c_i, 1 + n_i - c_i) and shows the arms with the highest draws, with a floor of one exploration slot in the group. Illustration: arm A with 3 clicks of 40 impressions has mean 4/42 = 0.095; arm B with 0 of 5 has mean 1/7 = 0.143 and a wider spread, so B is shown more until its impressions grow. The reward is a successful ask, not a click alone.

[0231] Technical effect. Server-side domain scoping keeps a user-facing surface from leaking operations content, and prior-art similarity alerts a user to a close existing filing before drafting.

### 34.2 Experimentation program

[0232] "Experiment" means a registered comparison of a treatment against a control on a named outcome metric over a fixed window. A registry table holds `id`, `name`, `hypothesis`, `kind`, `treatment`, `control`, `outcome_metric`, `baseline_date`, `status`, `owner` and `created_at`. A statistic table holds daily counts per exact path (`date`, `slug`, `requests`). A third table records per-post engagement. Kinds: content framing, version freshness, topic selection, writing style, and message framing, timing and subject lines.

[0233] Measurement rules. (a) Honest signal only: a platform's own invocation counts are self-health and are not cited as external traffic; honest signal is exact-path requests net of scanner traffic or an external record view and download count. (b) Aggregate over N: when a single signal is too small to reach significance, at least two independent signals are combined or the window is extended. (c) No fabrication: results are read from the stores. (d) Social tests follow platform and anti-burst rules. (e) Same window: treatment and control are measured over identical calendar windows.

[0234] Worked example from recorded measurements. On 2026-09-01 the treatment page had 3 requests per day and the control 4. Over a 14-day window under a Poisson assumption the counts are about 42 and 56. The standard error of their difference is sqrt(42 + 56) = 9.9, and the observed difference of 14 is 1.41 standard errors, below 1.96, so even a 33 percent difference would not be significant on that single signal in 14 days. This arithmetic is why rule (b) exists. Detecting a 50 percent lift from 4 per day at 80 percent power needs about (1.96 + 0.84)^2 x (4 + 6) / (4 - 6)^2 = 19.6 days (illustrative Poisson approximation).

[0235] Stop rules. In one embodiment the program stops an experiment on the first of: (1) the window closes (for example a 14-day close with a keep or adapt decision); (2) a guard metric breaches (for example an honest-traffic drop in both arms or an etiquette violation), which stops it at once; (3) futility, in which the sample needed for the target power exceeds a cap (for example 60 days); (4) a principal stop through a configuration row. Adaptation rule: a lever with a positive honest-signal delta on at least 2 metrics is promoted to standard practice for its surface; a neutral or negative lever is dropped with a rationale written to the registry. Rules (2) to (4) are illustrative.

[0236] Status: built, partially operating in the reference implementation. The registry and statistic tables were live; four experiments were registered (version freshness and social message framing running, posting time planned, an outreach subject line scheduled to start 2026-09-15); the weekly digest ran on Monday mornings; no source reports a completed readout in this chapter's material. No completed experiment readout is reported in this description.

### 34.3 Roadmap proposal and trusted sources

[0237] Section 5, step 314 describes a daily origination of one roadmap proposal per program under a threshold of open items. This subsection adds the validation path. A proposal is parsed from fields TITLE, WHY, code-task, code-anchor and EDIT; it is rejected unless (a) the target path is outside control-plane, governance and separated-plane paths, (b) the anchor is at least a minimum length (for example 20 characters) and occurs exactly once in the target, and (c) a proposal with the same normalized title is not open or closed in the last 30 days.
