A validated proposal becomes a work item whose `source` is on a trusted-source list; the list determines whether the item may take the merge-on-green and canary deploy path, while an item of any other source requires review. A daily cap bounds origination. A plus is reversible by construction and uses the reserved 20 percent of capacity; a roadmap proposal is sized as a feature. Status: built and operating for the proposal path described in section 5; the numeric validation limits shown here are illustrative.

### 34.4 Inferred direction from revealed preference

[0238] "Revealed preference" means a direction inferable from choices the principal made and avoided, as distinct from stated objectives. "Inferred direction" means an objective-weighting derived from revealed preference. The system keeps an `inferred_direction` table and a tick step that updates it before the queue is pulled.

[0239] Algorithm. At each tick (step "update inferred direction", ordered after recognition and before the queue pull) the processor counts, for each issue category, the items filed by the principal in the last 7 days. A category with 2 or more such items is written as a row with a confidence. In one embodiment confidence = min(1, 0.1 x count), so that 2 filings give 0.2 and the queue score is multiplied by (1 + 0.2), which matches the worked example of the execution queue (paragraph [0043]). Rows leave when the 7-day window no longer holds 2 items. The inference changes order only; it does not raise authority, change a limit, or alter a ratified objective.

[0240] Feedback. When an item in an inferred category is completed, the trust ledger records "inference acted on correctly" (+0.05) if the principal does not reverse it, and "inference misread" (-0.10) if the principal reverses or removes it, in the weights of the kernel. A metric counts inferred directions with no pulled item (`inferred_directions_unacted`), so that an inference that the driver does not use is visible. A repeated, stable direction is also an input to goal authoring (step 2002): it can seed a candidate that is then classified and anchored as in section 31.

[0241] Worked example (illustrative): in 7 days the principal files 3 items in `site` and 1 in `patent`. `site` has confidence 0.3 and `patent` no row; a `site` item with base score 18 becomes 18 x 1.3 = 23.4.

[0242] Status: built and operating in the reference implementation; the confidence formula and the feedback to goal authoring are illustrative or prophetic as stated.

[0243] Technical effect. The system aligns its work order to the principal's behavior without a questionnaire and without granting itself authority, which reduces principal prompts and keeps the signal inspectable as rows with a 7-day expiry.

[0244] Alternatives (prophetic). Revealed preference may be drawn from items the principal closes or reopens, edits to agent output, or gate cards approved quickly; categories may decay exponentially (for example half-life 3 days) in place of the 7-day window; a board embodiment may compute direction per member and require agreement of two.

## 35. Cost-optimized routing stack (FIG. 22)

[0245] This chapter extends the cost-aware model ladder of section 17 with the rung definitions, task-class rules, escalation conditions, caps and measurements that make it implementable. Terms: a "task class" means a named category of work (for example classification, extraction, digest synthesis, agent loop with tool calls, code writing, reader-facing drafting) for which one capability test and one validation rule are defined. A "model rung" means one entry in an ordered list of ways of producing an answer for a task class, from no model call at the first rung to a frontier model at the last. A "capability canary" means a short deterministic test run against a model for a task class that returns pass or fail. "Cost per verified outcome" means the spend attributed to a task divided by the number of tasks of that class that passed their validation gates (cost_usd divided by ok), as distinct from cost per token. "Resolved upstream" means the model that actually served a request after any alias, router or gateway indirection, as logged per request.

[0246] Technical effect. Spend on a model-backed fleet is dominated by repeated context and by retries, not by the price per token of any single call. Routing by capability first and price second, with a deterministic verifier deciding escalation, is expected to lower the cost per verified outcome without lowering the pass rate, and a fleet-wide cap with a free fallback prevents a budget breach from stopping work.

### 35.1 Rung definitions

[0247] In one embodiment the ladder has eight layers, descended in order, each entered only when the layer above cannot answer or has failed a measured check (steps 2204 to 2222 of FIG. 22; at step 2202 the task is received with its task class). Rungs L3 to L6 apply to the task classes for which the tenant's model floor (section 36) admits the model; for classes under a frontier-only floor the ladder reduces to L0 to L2 and L7.

| Layer | Name | Rule | Step of FIG. 22 |
|---|---|---|---|
| L0 | Deterministic handler | If the answer is computable (a SQL query over the ledgers, a regular expression, a probe, a static lookup, a template fill), compute it and make no model call | 2204, 2206 |
| L1 | Cache | Exact response cache with a time to live and invalidation on source change; key-value exact match for idempotent queries; semantic cache with a cosine threshold; reuse of stable prompt prefixes billed as cached input | 2208, 2210 |
| L2 | Capability gate | Keep only models whose canary passed for the task class; price is a tiebreaker, never the gate | 2212 |
| L3 | Cascade | Free tier first, then a cheap paid model, then a frontier model; move to the next rung only on a measured failure | 2218, 2220, 2222 |
| L4 | Ensemble | Sample N answers or propose-then-aggregate, only when components are free or cached | within 2218 |
| L5 | Distilled specialist | A small model or exemplar bank trained offline on trajectories from a frontier model for a recurring class | rung rows of 2212 |
| L6 | Draft then verify | A cheap model drafts; a frontier model repairs only when deterministic checks fail | 2218 to 2222 |
| L7 | Budget scheduler | Daily per-tier caps, an organization ceiling, a stated degradation order and a free fallback on cap hit | 2214, 2216 |

[0248] Status: built, partially operating in the reference implementation. In that table L0, L1, L2, L3, L4, L7 and context economics were each recorded as partial, L5 (distillation) and L6 (draft-verify) as absent, and the measurement view of cost per task as not yet built. L5 and L6 are therefore Status: prophetic (not yet built) in this specification, and the persisted capability matrix of L2 was a recorded gap at that date.

### 35.2 Capability before price

[0249] The capability gate (step 2212) rests on a measured observation: an agent or coding loop requires valid structured tool calls, and a cheaper model that does not emit them costs more through retries and truncation than the model it replaced. In one implementation four candidate models were recorded as not emitting tool calls for the operations agent loop while one cheap model did, so that routing by price alone silently broke the loop. The system therefore keeps a capability matrix keyed by (model, task class) holding a pass or fail result per canary (tool-call validity, schema conformance, context size) and routes only to models that pass. A suitable schema is:

```sql
CREATE TABLE model_task_matrix (
  task_class   TEXT NOT NULL,
  rung         INTEGER NOT NULL,        -- 0 = no model ... n = frontier
  model        TEXT NOT NULL,           -- resolved upstream identifier
  unit_cost    REAL NOT NULL,           -- per 1K input/output tokens, 0 for free tier
  canary_tool_calls INTEGER,            -- 1 pass, 0 fail, NULL untested
  canary_schema     INTEGER,
  ctx_tokens   INTEGER, max_out INTEGER,
  agreement    REAL,                    -- section 37 label agreement, NULL if not a gate model
  admitted_at  TEXT, probed_at TEXT,
  PRIMARY KEY (task_class, rung, model));
```

[0250] Selection algorithm (steps 2212 to 2218). The router reads the rows for the task class with all canary columns equal to 1 and agreement at or above the class gate where one applies; orders them by rung; and selects the lowest rung. Alternatively selection may weigh unit_cost against a measured pass rate (expected cost per verified outcome equals unit_cost times mean tokens divided by pass rate) rather than using rung order.

### 35.3 Cascade, verifier and escalation rules

[0251] In one implementation the cascade order was a free tier model served first for the operations path, then a cheap paid model, then a frontier model. Escalation (step 2222) is triggered only by a measured signal recorded in the attempt row: a schema or validator failure, a tool-call parse failure, empty content, truncation by length, or a confidence below a threshold. A deterministic verifier is what keeps the cascade cheap, because code can be verified by tests, schema validators and unit checks without a second model call. Pseudocode:

```
route(task):
  if det = handler(task.class): return det                 # 2204, 2206
  if hit = cache.get(key(task)) and not hit.invalidated:   # 2208, 2210
       return hit
  rungs = matrix.passing(task.class)                       # 2212
  r = 0
  loop:
    if cap_breached(task.class) or cap_breached(ORG):      # 2214
         r = max(r_free_if_any, r_cheaper(r)); log(step_down)   # 2216
    out = rungs[r].run(task)                               # 2218
    if verify(out, task.class): break                      # 2220
    attempts += 1
    if attempts > MAX_RETRIES or r == last: fail(task); return
    r += 1                                                 # 2222
  record(cost, task, r, attempts)                          # 2224
```

[0252] Example parameters: MAX_RETRIES of 2 per task; escalation only by one rung per failure; a failed task is filed as a work item rather than retried indefinitely. Alternatives: self-consistency voting among 3 to 5 free samples; escalation by a learned router in place of rules.

### 35.4 Caps, graceful degradation and the free fallback

[0253] Cap logic (steps 2214 and 2216). Each task class and the organization have a daily cap and a monthly ceiling, enforced both by the router (a count of spend in the gateway log joined by task) and by the platform gateway (a spend limit). The degradation order is: serve from cache; step to a free tier; step to a cheaper paid rung; queue the task for the next window. A paid-path failure or a cap hit does not end a task while a free path exists. In one implementation the gateway carried a monthly spend limit of 200 US dollars (illustrative) and a daily cost-per-impact guard performed detect, act and verify. Section 30.3 cites a different limit (90 dollars per 30 days), so cap figures are configuration and are examples.

[0254] Guard against inverted guards. A cost guard is installed only after the resolved upstream of the path it guards is probed, because a guard whose target is dearer than the status quo raises cost. In one incident on 2026-09-26 a gate that would have downgraded a free path to a paid model was caught by this rule before it shipped. No routing or cost change ships without a same-turn probe of the logged resolved upstream rather than the model constant in code.

### 35.5 Context economics and measurement

[0255] In one measurement, repeated contexts of 130,000 to 270,000 tokens dominated input cost, with some at 400,000 to 540,000. Levers in order: stable-prefix caching billed as cached input; compaction of old turns; structured conversation state in the relational database with a summary re-injected in place of raw history; retrieval of relevant slices instead of full context; and a bounded size on every tool result. Technical effect: input tokens per round stop growing with turn count. Measurement is by task class, joined on the gateway log (columns including upstream_model and job_id): cost per verified outcome; escalation rate (share of tasks reaching the frontier rung); cache hit rate; tool-call validity rate. A per-model health table records probe status. Status of the measurement view: prophetic as to the cost-per-task view; built as to the logged columns in the reference implementation.

[0256] Worked example (illustrative, for a class not under a model floor). A class has 100 tasks. A cheap model costs 0.002 per task and passes 60; a frontier model costs 0.02 per task and passes 98. Cheap-only cost per verified outcome is 0.2 / 60 = 0.0033 but leaves 40 failures. Cascade: 100 cheap attempts cost 0.2; the 40 failures go to the frontier rung and cost 0.8, of which 39 pass; total 1.0 for 99 verified, or 0.0101 per verified outcome, against frontier-only 2.0 / 98 = 0.0204. The cascade halves cost per verified outcome in this example and escalation rate is 0.40.

## 36. Model floor and model standards gate (FIG. 23)

[0257] "Model floor" means a minimum capability standard that a model must meet before it may be routed to a class of work, enforced by a scan of source and deployed artifacts. "Model standards gate" means a minimum-parameters standard (context window and maximum output) applied to every model advertised to or configured by any client, with a registry of exemptions. "Banned class" means a named family or size class of model excluded from the floor. Both standards are enforced by guard programs, so that a purge of a disallowed model is not reverted by a concurrent deployment.

### 36.1 Model floor

[0258] Rule. In one embodiment a tenant policy sets a model floor: a model that writes or executes code, or that produces reader-facing output, is of a frontier class, and a configured banned list excludes named small, legacy, distilled, small mixture-of-experts or quantized variants for the task classes the policy names. In one implementation the rule was adopted 2026-09-11. The banned list named one open-model family across five generations, several small base models, several 26B to 32B coder and distilled models, and any low-rank-adapted or quantized-fast variant; the approved list named four code models, three general models and a relay family treated as frontier scale. A comment mentioning a banned model for history is permitted if its line carries a marker string.

[0259] Justification measured in the field. A 70B-class quantized fast model used by a paper reviser to audit published research invented author attributions for work absent from the supplied source; fabricated attribution is the highest-cost failure in a publication pipeline. Separately the primary code and tool path of a router targeted a 30B mixture-of-experts model with about 3B active parameters while a frontier code model in the catalogue was unused; such a model does not hold a deep iterate-and-repair loop.

[0260] Enforcement algorithm (a guard run on the guard cadence). Step (a): enumerate repository sources of every worker and fetch the live deployed bundle of every worker through the platform API. Step (b): strip comment-only lines and declarations of the banned list itself, and skip lines with the marker. Step (c): match each remaining line against the banned patterns. Step (d): exit 0 if none match, otherwise exit 1 and file a work item per worker with the matching line. Canonical case: on 2026-09-11 an audit found 10 workers with live code-level banned models, including a router and an agent gateway; earlier one-off purges had been reverted by concurrent redeploys because nothing checked. Status: built and operating in the reference implementation.

### 36.2 Model standards gate

[0261] Rule. Every model advertised to or configured by a client carries maximum output at or above 32,768 tokens and a context window at or above 128,000 tokens, except an operations executor whose context floor is above 64,000 (actual 1,048,576). A model that cannot meet the floors is retired from the catalog or registered in an exemption registry with per-model evidence of the platform-native cap. Silent noncompliance is a hard finding. The registry in one implementation held entries such as a 24,000-context, 16,384-output model; two 32,768-context, 16,384-output models; an 80,000-context model with compliant output; a vision model with 4,096 output; a short-form advisor limited to 350 requested tokens; and an embedding model.

[0262] Verification recipe (steps (e) to (h)). Step (e): for each server, read the health version and fetch the deployed bundle, then search for the constants for default maximum output, answer cap, reasoning output cap and model context. Step (f): for each client store (provider settings file, a database table of model configurations, a backup of a chat client's configuration), read back per-model maximum output. Step (g): run the catalog guard, which compares each catalog entry to the registry with floors ctx >= 128,000 and maxOut >= 32,768 and checks that every internal model appears. Step (h): record a claim sheet row (claim, evidence, confidence, status). Truncation was found on both server and client layers, so all layers are verified after every model change. In one run the server constants were: default and answer maximum output 65,536, tool round and tool result caps 32,768, relay clamp 32,768, model context 1,048,576. A cap-halving retry reduces a requested ceiling by half when the platform returns a 400 and a context-aware target keeps requests in range; /v1/models advertises honest values only. Status: built and operating in the reference implementation.

[0263] The exemption registry of 2026-09-08 lists models (for example a 30B mixture-of-experts and a 32B coder) that the floor of 2026-09-11 bans; the later floor supersedes those entries.

### 36.3 Minimum capability per task class

[0264] The floor and gate define a lower bound across all classes; the capability matrix of section 35.2 defines the bound per class. In one embodiment a model is eligible for a class only if it is above the floor, meets the gate (or is exempt for that class with evidence), has passed the class canary, and for a class used as a gate on the organization's decisions has met the agreement threshold of section 37. Illustratively, for classes under a frontier-only floor: drafting and code writing, frontier class only; classification and extraction, any admitted model; agent loop, only a model with a recorded tool-call pass.

## 37. Fleet calibration and model admission (FIG. 23)

[0265] "Calibration of infrastructure" means scheduled self-generated probe traffic that measures latency and availability and adjusts configuration within an allowlist. "Calibration of predictions" means comparing the confidence stated by predictions with their realized hit rate. "Calibration of model decisions" means comparing a decision model's per-question answers with the organization's own labels. "Agreement" means the fraction of labelled questions on which the model's answer equals the organization's label.

### 37.1 Infrastructure calibration and stress testing

[0266] In one implementation a calibrator worker ran three schedules: daily at 03:00 UTC, a weekly stress run (Sunday 03:30 UTC, the daily set plus an adversarial input battery, 8-way burst concurrency and plan-weight tuning) and a monthly run (04:00 on the first, the stress run plus retention cleanup of 90 days of metrics, 30 days of resolved anomalies and 7 days of calibration objects). The static probe set covered 8 HTTP probes through service bindings and hosted sites, the relational database, the object store and the vector index. A missed schedule is recorded as a schedule gap and the next fire recovers. Tables fleet_cal_runs, metrics, baselines, anomalies, actions, learnings and state hold results; probe traffic is self-generated and never counted as external usage.

[0267] Self-checks. Each run writes a verdict_score from completeness, integrity and plausibility checks with audit_json; a simulate mode injects an extreme input to show the detector fires. Each probe retries once. Baselines are exponential moving averages with variance-adaptive thresholds (illustratively, EMA mean 120 ms and deviation 15 ms give a threshold of mean plus 3 deviations, 165 ms; a 240 ms run is an anomaly). Autonomous adjustments are limited to an allowlist of reversible, binding-only actions (tune-threshold, tune-plan, publish-config, retention-cleanup); each has before and after JSON, is verified on the next run, and is reverted with an audit row and a learning if the metric regressed. A real anomaly auto-resolves when its probe is clean; a simulated anomaly is resolved manually after verification. Evidence: on 2026-09-04 a daily and a stress run each scored 1.0, all adversarial cases survived, a simulated anomaly was detected at severity high, and a real vector-probe anomaly auto-resolved after a fix. Status: built and operating in the reference implementation.

### 37.2 Calibration of predictions and decisions

[0268] Prediction calibration (extends section 8). Predictions stored in the decision-prediction ledger carry a stated confidence p. At scoring, group predictions into confidence bins (for example 0.5 to 0.6, ... 0.9 to 1.0) per decision type and compute for each bin the hit rate h. Calibration error is the count-weighted mean of |p_bin - h_bin|; a Brier score is the mean of (p - outcome)^2. Illustrative arithmetic: if 40 predictions in the 0.8 to 0.9 bin (mean 0.85) were matched 28 times, h = 0.70 and the gap 0.15 lowers the confidence cap applied to that decision type by 0.15 (the confidence cap is the highest stated confidence that the decision type may carry when gating autonomous action). Technical effect: stated confidence that gates autonomous action is replaced by an empirically corrected one. In the reference implementation 1,056 predictions were scored with 73 percent matched (section 21), which is an aggregate hit rate, not a binned calibration; the binned computation is Status: prophetic (not yet built).

[0269] Decision-model calibration (extends section 17). A decision model that returns a probability per answer may be used as a gate only after the pipeline of FIG. 23 shows its agreement with the organization's labels at or above a gate for the class. In one implementation a candidate decision model agreed with the labels in 0.17 of cases against a gate of 0.7 and was not adopted for that task. For context, random answering among k options agrees with probability 1/k, so 0.17 is near the random rate for six options (illustrative).

### 37.3 Model-admission pipeline

[0270] The pipeline runs per (candidate model, task class) and is, in one embodiment, the path by which a row enters model_task_matrix. Step 2302: a candidate model is proposed, by a person, by a capability scan or by an escalation analysis. Step 2304: the floor check of section 36 (not banned, context and output limits met or exempt); failure is rejected at step 2306 with the reason recorded. Step 2308: capability canary (tool-call validity, schema conformance, context size) against fixtures of the class. Step 2310: label agreement check: draw a stratified sample of N questions from the organization's labelled store (for example N between 100 and 500; fewer than 30 is insufficient and the check returns inconclusive), obtain the model's answer per question, compute agreement as matches divided by N and a lower confidence bound (for example a Wilson 95 percent bound). Step 2312: the gate compares the lower bound with the class gate (example range 0.6 to 0.85; 0.7 in the one measurement). Failure is step 2314: the model is rejected for that class, the measured agreement is stored and it may still be admitted for other classes. Success is step 2316: a rung row is written. Step 2318: monitoring, by scheduled canaries, baselines of section 37.1, a shadow sample in which a fraction (for example 5 percent) of production decisions is also labelled by a person or the stronger rung to compute rolling agreement, and cost per verified outcome. Step 2320: if rolling agreement falls below the gate minus a margin (for example 0.05), if the canary fails twice in a row or if the floor list changes, step 2322 demotes or removes the row, routing reverts to the next passing rung, an audit row records the reason, and the model may re-enter at step 2302 after a change.

```sql
CREATE TABLE model_admission (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY, model TEXT, task_class TEXT,
  stage TEXT,            -- floor|canary|agreement|admitted|demoted|rejected
  n INTEGER, agree REAL, lower_bound REAL, gate REAL,
  decided_at TEXT, reason TEXT, evidence TEXT);
```

[0271] Alternatives: agreement per question class; admission by a second-family judge where labels are scarce, with a human check on a sample; cohort rollout at 1 percent, then 10 percent of traffic; a gate on calibration error in place of agreement. Status: built in part (floor guard, standards guard, canary probes, agreement check observed once: section 17); the persisted matrix, shadow sampling and demotion step are Status: prophetic (not yet built).

## 38. Executors, code-only mode and per-executor trust

[0272] "Executor" has the meaning of section 1. "Executor adapter" means a module presenting an executor of any kind through one interface. "Code-only execution mode" means a routed mode of an agent endpoint in which a model is restricted to writing, running and verifying code with a restricted tool set. These elements keep the principle of section 20 that no step waits for an interactive session.

### 38.1 Executor worker and scheduler

[0273] In one implementation a scheduler worker and an executor worker implement dynamic scheduling in two components, as in sections 27.3 and 27.4, which section 38.1 uses. The differences are that an ai task selects among three named tiers, value, standard and premium (default value), and stores its output to 2,000 characters.

[0274] Technical effect: a task is added, disabled or retimed by a database write rather than a deployment, every execution has a ledger row, and a malformed record is rejected before the canonical store. Failure cases: a dispatch error leaves a row at queued status and is only returned in the tick result; and because the executor updates the latest run row by task and cron name, two overlapping runs of the same task can contend for one row. Mitigations: a section 13 claim before dispatch and a reaper for stale queued rows. Status: built in the reference implementation.

### 38.2 Code-only execution mode

[0275] Rule. An endpoint that serves chat, operations and code from one route can instead classify each request as code, ops or chat and route it, so that code requests are served by a model with a code-only contract and code toolset while the other traffic is unchanged. In an audit of 1,390 logged requests, code execution was invoked 79 times of 2,582 tool invocations (3.1 percent), and 97 percent of traffic was operations introspection, a memory pipeline, chat and content creation, so a global code-only gate would break the memory pipeline, the conversation compressor and the main agent.

[0276] Classifier. A deterministic function with no model call, applied to the last user turn and never to injected system context (which would label every request of some clients as code). Code signals are weighted and threshold-gated: explicit intent phrases (run, write a function, implement, fix this code, debug, refactor, write a test, deploy, commit); code artifacts (code fences, import statements, keywords, file extensions); and an explicit domain hint in the body or a header. Ops signals are tool and resource names. The default is chat. Bias rule: when evidence is ambiguous, the class is ops or chat, because a code request misrouted to ops still works and the converse breaks the memory pipeline. Router: code goes to a code-only prompt, a code model and the code toolset; ops goes to the existing prompt and the full operations toolset (31 tools in the implementation); chat goes to the general path without tools. The class is logged to a domain column of the request log for every request.

[0277] What an executor may do in code mode (contract C1 to C8, summarized). It returns executed results first with at most a one-line summary; it executes only on the server side, using a sandboxed runtime and a virtual file workspace, and never emits code, shell commands or tool-call payloads for the client to run; it loops plan, write, run, verify and report in one turn without asking permission; its tools are limited to run-code, workspace read, write, list and delete, repository read, file write and pull request, and web fetch and search, with operations tools excluded; every claim of done carries the actual output as evidence, and an error is reported with its exact text; it states at least one concrete failure mode; execution is bounded, and long or CPU-heavy work goes through an asynchronous job path. Sandboxing: all execution occurs in isolated serverless runtimes at no marginal cost and the workspace is an object-store backed virtual filesystem, so no local machine is touched and a client such as a phone that cannot run code is still served. No-interactive-session operation: because the executor never hands work to the client, the mode runs from the scheduler, the queue and the claim ledger without a person present.

[0278] Verification probes: a canonical arithmetic request returns the exact product 83,810,205 with finish reason stop and no client-side execution; a code-shaped prompt returns domain code, the code model and executed output with no prose; a memory-pipeline prompt returns domain chat; an ops prompt returns domain ops; and the domain column is populated after each probe. Rollout: implement in place with no new endpoint, verify the probes, watch the domain distribution for 48 hours, then expand the toolset. Fallback ladder (for a class not under a model floor): a free code model, a paid code model, a general frontier model. Code-model quality against a frontier standard and classifier precision were unmeasured. Status: prophetic (not yet built) for the classifier and router in the reference implementation; built and operating for the server-side-only enforcement that the draft recorded as already landed, with zero hybrid-family rows since 2026-09-09.

### 38.3 Executor adapters behind one claim ledger

[0279] "Adapter interface" means the functions an executor kind implements so the dispatcher and ledgers treat all kinds alike: describe() returning capabilities, supported task classes, model, cost class and trust key; claim(item) taking a claim per section 13; run(item) returning a result envelope with evidence; and report() writing outcome, cost and duration. Kinds: (a) an open model on the platform, driven through a tool loop of the worker of section 38.1; (b) a hosted coding agent that opens pull requests, mapped by an adapter that creates the work as an issue with the doer line and takes a claim on the path; (c) an interactive session of a person or a coding assistant, which acts only as a normal claimant and which no scheduled step waits for; (d) a container executor running the same worker code under a job scheduler. All kinds write through the canonical change path of section 12, so the claim fence rejects a stale holder whichever kind it is. In the reference implementation more than 400 changes were merged by a hosted coding agent and more than 600 by the system's own code loop, coordinated through one claim ledger (section 21). Status: built and operating for the hosted-agent and own-loop executors in the reference implementation; the uniform describe/claim/run/report interface is Status: prophetic (not yet built).

[0280] Dispatch: the dispatcher reads queue items in priority order, filters adapters whose describe() lists the class and whose trust key is at or above the item's required rung, orders them by cost class and dispatches the first available. If none qualifies, the item stays queued with a reason; it is not handed to the principal unless identity-bound.

### 38.4 Per-executor trust

[0281] Section 9 keys trust by (surface, action class). Per-executor trust adds the executor as a third key, an option noted in section 9, so a new model or agent starts at the prior and earns scope on its own record. Schema: executor_trust(executor_id, surface, action_class, ok, fail, trust_score, ladder_position, refreshed_at). Computation: trust_score = (ok + 5) / (ok + fail + 10) as in section 9. Worked example (illustrative): a new open-model executor with ok 6 and fail 2 on (repository, merge) scores 11/18 = 0.61, autonomous rung for that pair, while the same pair for a long-running executor with ok 30 and fail 35 scores 0.467 and its merges take the added-review path. The effective rung for a dispatch is the minimum of the executor rung and the (surface, action class) rung, so a trusted executor does not widen a distrusted surface. A model removed at step 2322 has its executor rows frozen, not deleted, so history is preserved. Alternatives: a lower confidence bound; decay toward the prior while idle; trust transferred between model versions of one family at a discount (for example half the counts). Status: prophetic (not yet built), the per-pair ledger on which it builds being built (section 9).

## 39. Audit-and-act procedure with verification, correction and capstone documents (FIG. 24)

### 39.1 Terms

[0282] "Audit-and-act procedure" means a repeatable procedure in which an agent process reads live state of a managed system, records numbered findings, acts on findings that survive re-testing, and records the outcome of each action. "Finding" means a record having an identifier within the audit, a claim about the managed system, the evidence for the claim (a query, a probe result, or a source-file hash, with the time at which it was read), a falsifier (an observation that would show the claim false), and a state. In one embodiment the state is one of `open`, `verified`, `corrected`, `retracted`, `pending` and `closed`. "Verification document" means a document that re-tests each finding of an earlier document by a path independent of the one that produced it. "Correction document" means a document that supersedes a named part of an earlier document, states what was claimed, what test showed it wrong, and what still stands. "Retraction" means a correction document that withdraws a finding in full. "Capstone" means a document that states the structural cause shared by several findings. "Measured clock" means a timestamp read from the managed system in the same statement or call that produces the evidence, as distinct from a clock value assumed or inferred by the agent.

[0283] Status: performed in the reference implementation on one occasion (an audit session), with its documents; the schema of section 39.3 and an automatic detector are prophetic.

[0284] Technical effect: because each finding carries a falsifier, is re-tested by an independent path, and is superseded by a dated document rather than overwritten when wrong, the audit trail reports the error rate of the audit itself, and downstream actions (closing tickets, deploying, retiring workers) are taken only on findings that reproduced.

### 39.2 Procedure

[0285] FIG. 24 shows the procedure. At step 2402 an audit agent reads live state of the managed system: for example database queries over alert, event, issue, queue and probe tables, service-binding health probes, and file hashes of canonical source against deployed source, together with a clock read from the database (for example `strftime('%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%SZ','now')`). At step 2404 the agent writes one numbered finding per claim, with evidence, falsifier and state `open`. A first audit may carry headline population counts that separate populations the alert stream conflates (for example 931 alert rows from one source, 3 open issues, and 12 of 55 workers probed healthy with 43 never probed).

[0286] At step 2406 a verification document is written, by the same or another agent, that re-tests each finding by an independent path, and is named by ordinal (for example `VERIFICATION-2` to `VERIFICATION-13`). The independent path is chosen so that a shared fault cannot produce the same answer: a source claim against live database state; a database claim against a second table or a service-binding probe. At step 2408 the verification document sets each finding to `verified` (the claim reproduces) or to a not-reproduced result. A finding that does not reproduce proceeds at step 2410 to a correction document or retraction, which names the superseded section, quotes the claim, states the control experiment that falsified it, and lists claims that are unchanged. The original document is not edited; both remain, so that the history of what was believed is retained.

[0287] A finding that reproduces proceeds at step 2412 to action: a source patch, a tombstone file that removes a shadowing source, a dispatch to an executor, an annotation of a record in place (the original value preserved in the `reason` column with a flag appended), or a change to a registry. At step 2414 the realization gate of section 40 tests whether the effect of the action was observed in the managed system. When it was not (the action exists only in a repository or in a staged patch), step 2416 sets the finding to `pending`, records the deciding query and the earliest time at which it can decide, and returns to step 2406 to re-test at that time. When it was observed, step 2418 writes a capstone that groups findings by structural cause and checks the cause against independent rosters (section 42.3). Step 2420 feeds the failure modes found into the failure mode register and the silent-drop reconciliation of FIG. 25, and the loop returns to step 2402. A correction document also reaches step 2418, because a retraction changes which causes the capstone may assert.

### 39.3 Document schema

[0288] In one embodiment each document is a Markdown file under version control with a header (`Date`, `Supersedes`, `Scope`) and tabulated findings; a database embodiment stores rows:

```sql
CREATE TABLE audit_finding (
  audit_id TEXT, finding_no TEXT, claim TEXT NOT NULL, evidence_query TEXT,
  evidence_read_at TEXT,            -- measured clock
  falsifier TEXT NOT NULL, state TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'open',
  superseded_by TEXT,               -- document path + section
  PRIMARY KEY (audit_id, finding_no));
CREATE TABLE audit_document (
  path TEXT PRIMARY KEY, kind TEXT CHECK (kind IN
    ('audit','verification','correction','retraction','capstone')),
  supersedes TEXT, created_at TEXT);
```

### 39.4 Worked examples from one audit session

[0289] The following examples are taken from the audit of a worker fleet on 2026-09-13 (an audit session of a hosted coding agent with read access to the fleet's relational database). They are included because each shows a distinct way in which the procedure detects a fault in the managed system or in the audit itself. Where a claim was later withdrawn, both the claim and its withdrawal are given.

[0290] Example A, alert storm and its undeployed fix. Finding H1: one worker (`worker-A`) accounted for 931 alert rows all-time and 72 in the 24 hours to 14:08Z, every one reporting that it filed nothing. The hourly counts for the first 15 hours of the day were 6, 5, 2, 7, 4, 7, 3, 6, 5, 7, 5, 4, 4, 4, 3 (sum 72). Canonical source declared version 0.5.5 with alert deduplication; production still emitted the earlier wording. The verification document tested the claim directly: canonical source created a table `pipeline_state` at the top of each run, a query for that table returned `no such table`, and therefore the deployed build predated the fix. The cadence of 2 to 7 per hour independently excluded both later behaviors (alert only on a new ticket; re-notify at most every 6 hours).

[0291] Example B, the monitor that was claimed silent and was not (retraction). An audit document claimed that the dashboard worker that writes `fleet_probe_log` had stopped at 14:16:31Z, missed four consecutive `*/15` cron fires, and left a 69-minute blackout. The retraction states the cause: the agent read an attachment header `15:16 (UTC+02:00)` and treated 15:16 as UTC; the true time was 14:30:10Z, so every elapsed figure was inflated by two hours. The real gap was 13.65 minutes, one normal interval. A test fixed the record: a GET to the dashboard's refresh route advanced `fleet_dashboard_state.updated_at` from 14:16:39.546Z to 14:30:00.350Z and added 20 probe rows. The correction rule that followed is the measured clock of step 2402: a time-based claim is computed from a timestamp read from the managed system in the same query, and a timestamp bearing an offset is converted before use.

[0292] Example C, probe coverage collapse (verification that changed the finding). A parent document inferred that the prober had died because 73 of 83 names were frozen at one timestamp. The correction found one writer, about 21,070 rows (21,068 at a later reading), written continuously. Hourly distinct probed names were 81, 81, 81, 82, then 10 every hour from 2026-09-12T10Z for 27 consecutive hours, with a discrete transition at 09:15:45Z. The fault is therefore a coverage collapse, not a liveness collapse, and 43 of 55 workers had no liveness probe (the earlier 15 UNMEASURED workers were the same blind spot). The mechanism was recorded as not determined, because two candidates (a live-script list of about 10 names filtering out 34 of 44 probes, or a registry cut to bindings only) fit the data and could not be separated from the endpoint used.

[0293] Example D, fix-ordering hazard. The obvious fix for Example C was to deploy the repository registry (44 health probes). A set difference over the repository's 27 scheduled entries and the 40 workers in the activity table showed that the deployed roster (40) was a strict superset, and that deploying the repository file would remove measurement of 13 workers carrying 4,372 of 8,698 measured requests per 24 hours (4,372 / 8,698 = 50.3 percent), including the busiest worker (1,656). The prescribed procedure: take the deployed registry as base, add back the 34 missing probes, and verify before and after that distinct probed names per hour exceed 80 and that the activity table still reports 40 workers per snapshot.

[0294] Example E, executor that accounts for nothing. The deployed dashboard's loop endpoint reported `scanned 25, executed 0, failed 0, needs_human 0, no_action 0`. The outcome buckets sum to 0 against 25 scanned, so 25 items were unaccounted. All 8 tracked signals showed the same ledger row: `attempts 1`, `dispatch_state dispatched`, `last_action stale-escalated`, `last_verified null`, `closed_at null`, and occurrences between 87 and 127. A related table held 34 dispatch rows, all `queued`, with execution attempts summing to 5 and no transitions. The finding is stated as the invariant `executed + failed + needs_human + no_action == scanned`; until it holds, no detected alert can be shown to be resolved. The detector and the dispatcher were operating; the executor was absent. The same document flagged in place two contradictions in a census of an earlier session (6 of 35 PRODUCTIVE rows with no measurement), appending the flag to the `reason` column and preserving the original judgment.

[0295] Example F, the inert remediation loop. The fleet's self-assessment (`fleet_dashboard_state.state_json`) listed 8 tracked issues (3 error, 5 warning), each with `auto_actionable: true` and a `remediation.suggested_action` (for example `gateway-classify`, `queue-drain`, `worker-rollback`), while its loop block reported `scanned: 25, executed: 0`. Detection was comprehensive and automated; correction was not running.

[0296] Example G, repository behind production. The repository declared `VERSION = '1.0.18'` for the dashboard worker while production reported `1.5.1`, and the repository's state builder lacked the `loop`, `coverage` and `verdict` fields present in production. The remediation loop's source was therefore not at the searched path. This mirror of the usual drift (canonical ahead of production) also hides the worker from a comparator that classifies it `deployed-ahead` and skips it.

[0297] Example H, deploy blind spot and auto-heal disabled. A capstone attributed the storm fix's failure to ship to the worker's absence from the deploy machinery: zero rows in the drift table (1,708 rows, 62 distinct workers), zero in the deploy table, and none in the 55-name service registry. A later verification found the healer's flag `auto_heal` set to `0` at 14:15:02Z, so no drift row had been written since 14:05:46; a test described as "pending the next scan" was pending a flag. Further verifications showed that the scan set is keyed by the registry (scan of 55 equals 55 registry rows) and that the worker had been dropped by a consolidation (its probe record changed from `cf-api-list: script live` to `script missing from live CF list` between 2026-09-11T16:30Z and 09-12T08:15Z), so registering it would have been the wrong remedy. The emitter of the alerts remained unidentified; a worker merged into a hub may continue to run under its own name constant. The 55-name service registry named here is distinct from the 80-row scan registry of section 27 and was read at a different date.

### 39.5 The audit's own errors

[0298] The documents record the errors of the audit explicitly. Table 39-1 lists corrections found during the same session (a selection; a daily-brief contradiction from pairing two producers by `MAX(ts)`, and three prematurely closed tickets, were also corrected). No rate is claimed because the number of findings tested differs by document.

| Claim | Why wrong | How found |
|---|---|---|
| Probe path wrong: `/health` 404 on three workers | Every `*.workers.dev` fetch from the endpoint returned 404 (16 of 16) while 2 of 2 other hosts returned 200 and service-binding probes returned 200 for the same workers | Control experiment, host-level |
| 40 failing `v2-drain` rows masked as ok | Population was 19 rows with the failing payload; 40 was all kinds | Prior session's per-payload count |
| One degraded model row | A `LIMIT 20` truncated a 25-row table; 5 rows degraded | Re-query with `GROUP BY` |
| No rearm alerts since 12:00 | Timestamps stored in two formats; space (0x20) sorts below `T` (0x54), so the predicate excluded every row of the producer | Re-run with the space form |
| "No deploy route exists"; "deployer unreadable" | Route is commit then hourly scan; deployer was unregistered, not unreadable | Reading the resolver source |
| Register the worker (capstone remedy) | Worker had been dropped; also registry version column would suppress the heal | Later verifications 10 and 11 |
| Acute monitor outage | Offset time zone treated as UTC | Measured clock |
| Prober died | Freeze was coverage, not liveness | Single-writer check |
| Test pending next scan | Pending on `auto_heal = 0` | State table read |

[0299] Technical effect: each correction in Table 39-1 would have driven a wrong action if left standing (retiring a healthy worker, deploying a registry that blinds half the traffic, registering a worker the fleet had retired).

### 39.6 Alternative embodiments

[0300] In one alternative a second model of a different family performs step 2406 so that the verifier does not share the author's reasoning. In another each finding carries a re-test query and a due time, and a correction record is written automatically when the re-run count differs by more than a tolerance (for example 5 percent). In another, findings are ordered by blast radius (the share of measured traffic or workers that a failure reaches before detection).

## 40. Map-territory realization gate and blocker red-team (FIG. 24)

### 40.1 Terms

[0301] "Map" means the representation of the managed system held in a repository, a registry, a staged patch or a document. "Territory" means the managed system's state as read from the running system. "Realization gate" means a check, at step 2414, that a claimed fix or claimed fact has been observed in the territory and not only in the map. "Claim state" means one of `code-only`, `staged`, `inferred` and `observed`. "Deciding query" means a statement whose result decides whether a pending claim holds, with a stated pass condition and a stated fail condition. "Blocker" means a claim that work cannot proceed.

### 40.2 Realization gate

[0302] Status: built, partially operating. The policy text governs claims in a research pipeline: a claim is `map`, `bridge` or `territory`; it moves toward territory only through a declared bridge carrying a measurement protocol, a null model and a falsification condition, and a claim lacking the triple is demoted one level. The audits of 2026-09-13 apply the same discipline to operational claims by practice. Automatic enforcement of claim states by a program is prophetic (not yet built).

[0303] The gate is applied by the following steps. (1) A fix is recorded with a claim state: `code-only` when the change exists only in a repository, `staged` when a patcher or tombstone is committed but has not been applied, `inferred` when the territory shows the expected effect indirectly, and `observed` when a deciding query reads the changed state in production. (2) The agent writes a deciding query, a pass condition, a fail condition and a null expectation (what production would show if the fix were absent). (3) The finding may be closed only when the claim state is `observed`; `inferred` closes with a label `inferred` and a scheduled re-test; `code-only` and `staged` leave the finding `pending`. (4) The claim states map to the policy's levels: `code-only` to map, `staged` and `inferred` to bridge, `observed` to territory, and the demotion rule applies when the deciding query or null expectation is missing.

[0304] Worked example. A fix for backlog executor version 1.2.7 was committed to both mirror paths of the worker. The deciding evidence was a deploy row `id 75 | worker-B | 1.2.7 -> 1.2.8 | ok=1 | 14:02:44`, preceded by drift row 1696 at 14:02:39 showing `canonical-ahead`. The claim state is `observed`. A second fix, replacing a corrupt multipart body at a mirror path of another worker with a `404:` tombstone, showed 25 hourly failures, the last at `2026-09-13 07:02:38`, and none after; the claim state is `inferred` from silence, because the absence of failures is also what a stopped scanner would produce. A third fix, for a research-execution worker, was `staged`: the next scan was predicted to record a `source_path` ending in `worker.js`, and the deciding query is

```sql
SELECT id, source_path, canonical_version, note FROM fleet_drift_report
 WHERE worker='worker-C' ORDER BY id DESC LIMIT 1;
-- pass: source_path ends in worker.js; fail: still names deployed-current.worker.js
```

[0305] That query could not decide while `auto_heal` was `0` (Example H). A fourth case shows a per-component state: a provenance guard script could not be run from the endpoint, so its decision logic was re-implemented and executed in isolated compute (11 of 11 branches passing), and the correction states that this verified the logic and not the file, whose filesystem walk and workflow wiring remained unexecuted.

[0306] Technical effect: closing items on repository evidence leaves production unchanged and produces recurrence of the same alert. The gate limits premature closures to those carrying a visible `inferred` label with a re-test date.

### 40.3 Blocker red-team

[0307] Status: built and operating as a recognition rule and a trust deduction in the reference implementation; the audit examples are evidence of the failure the rule addresses.

[0308] "Surface" means any distinct path to the same outcome. A blocker is accepted as proven only after every enumerated surface has a recorded test with its result. The steps are: (1) enumerate surfaces for the outcome (a deploy has: commit to a source repository with a scanner, a direct deployer call, a manual command from a machine holding the account credential, a registry insert that brings the worker into scan scope, and a staged patcher runner); (2) test each, recording the call and the response; (3) mark the blocker `proven` only when each surface returned a refusal attributable to the surface; (4) otherwise mark `unproven` and execute the first surface that did not refuse, or construct a new one; (5) record the blocker with the surfaces tested so that a later audit can add surfaces.

[0309] Worked example. The audit asserted three blockers. (a) "No deploy route exists." Surface test: the deployer's resolver read in full (24,761 bytes) showed order R2 canonical if fresher than 30 minutes, then four repository paths (`deployed-current.worker.js` before `worker.js`, in each of two repositories), then stale R2. A route existed (commit, then hourly scan); the blocker was the shadow of candidate (a) over candidate (c). (b) "Registration is impossible from this endpoint." The registry is a table in a bound database and the endpoint held a write tool; the claim was untested and wrong. (c) "The canonical source bucket is unreachable." The prefix returned 0 objects in each of four bound buckets, so the bucket is a fifth, unbound one; this blocker survived with its four tested surfaces recorded. Two of three blockers fell on red-team. In a red-team round on a policy amendment, the amendment is generated, challenged by an adversary position per defect class, and adopted in the same output when it survives (section 25.1, step E4).

[0310] In alternatives the red-team is run by a model of a different family, or enumerates surfaces from a register of surfaces per outcome built from prior audits.

## 41. Failure mode register, detectors, remediation contracts and loop breaker (FIG. 25)

### 41.1 Terms

[0311] "Failure mode register" means a table of named failure modes, each with a detector, a threshold and a severity. "Detector" means a database statement or probe that returns a count, a ratio or a set, evaluated on a schedule against the managed system and compared with a threshold. "Remediation contract" means a record attached to an item that states the responsible role, the doer, the trigger that starts remediation, the closing probe that decides success, a retry bound, and a deadline. "Outcome bucket" means one of the dispositions of a scanned item (`executed`, `failed`, `needs_human`, `no_action`). "Loop breaker" means a procedure that files a root-cause item when the same signature repeats.

### 41.2 Register and detectors

[0312] Status: built and operating in the reference implementation. The schema below is illustrative; entries named in Table 41-1 are drawn from failures recorded in the audits and are registrations that the procedure of section 39 proposes at step 2420, not a list of rows read from the live table.

```sql
CREATE TABLE failure_modes (
  id TEXT PRIMARY KEY, name TEXT, surface TEXT,
  detector_sql TEXT NOT NULL, fire_when TEXT NOT NULL,   -- e.g. 'value < 0.8*expected'
  severity INTEGER, contract_id TEXT, first_seen TEXT, last_fired TEXT,
  status TEXT DEFAULT 'armed');
CREATE TABLE failure_mode_hits (
  fm_id TEXT, ts TEXT, value REAL, expected REAL, fired INTEGER, evidence TEXT);
```

[0313] At step 2502 the register is read. At step 2504 each detector runs on a schedule (for example hourly) and writes one `failure_mode_hits` row whether or not it fires, so that an armed detector that stops producing rows is itself detectable. At step 2506 a detector fires when its value crosses its threshold. Table 41-1 gives examples with arithmetic.

| Id | Detector (summary) | Fires when | Audit instance |
|---|---|---|---|
| FM-COVERAGE | distinct names in `fleet_probe_log` in the last hour | below 0.8 times names in the registry | 10 names against 82 observed earlier; 10/82 = 0.12 |
| FM-MASKED | rows with status `ok` whose structured payload has `ok` false or a named exception | count above 0 | 19 `v2-drain` rows with the failing payload |
| FM-UNBOUNDED-RETRY | same (target, error) in the deploy ledger without a state change | 3 attempts | 26 and 25 attempts for two targets |
| FM-SHADOW | two candidate sources for one worker with differing hashes and a stale earlier candidate | any | stale `deployed-current` over fresher `worker.js` |
| FM-UNACCOUNTED | scanned minus sum of outcome buckets | above 0 | 25 - 0 = 25 |
| FM-FROZEN | measured now minus newest write of a table, divided by its interval | above 2 | newest probe at 14:16:31Z against now 14:30:10Z: 13.65 min / 15 = 0.91, not fired |
| FM-ROSTER | symmetric difference between registry, scan set, probe set and emitter set | any | emitter name not in 55-name registry |
| FM-STALE-PREDICATE | an alarm predicate's population differs from the population it names | any | alarm on 496 stuck `new` while live `new` was 0 |

[0314] The detector for FM-FROZEN takes the current time in the same statement, as in the following, so that the clock error of Example B of section 39 cannot occur:

```sql
SELECT (julianday('now') - julianday(MAX(replace(ts,' ','T')))) * 1440.0 AS age_min,
       15.0 AS interval_min
FROM fleet_probe_log;  -- fire when age_min / interval_min > 2
```

[0315] A detector can itself be wrong. A classifier that marked an event `error` on the substring `failed` classified `{"queued":0,"failed":2,"published":19}` (where 2 is a count) as a failure; the corrected classifier matches structured failure (`"ok":false`, a named exception, an error-code prefix). A detector whose firings are confirmed by verification documents in under a threshold share (for example 50 percent over 10 firings) is set to `probation`.

[0316] Because the monitor cannot detect its own stop, one detector is hosted in a different worker than the one that writes the probe table, and the probe-writing worker is checked by a measured-clock freshness query from that worker. Status of the external liveness detector: prophetic (not yet built); the audit that proposed it was retracted as to its trigger event, while the design rule stands.

### 41.3 Remediation contract

[0317] Status: built, partially operating. The fleet's issue records carry `id, schema, severity_rank, category, owner, auto_actionable, remediation.suggested_action, occurrences` and a link to an external tracker, and kernel rule OWNER-NO-LOOSE-ENDS-1 gives issues a doer, a trigger and a probe; the audit of 2026-09-13 found that the executor consuming these records booked no outcomes (section 42.1). The retry bound and the deadline are prophetic (not yet built) as fields of the contract.

[0318] At step 2508 a firing detector opens an item whose contract is, in one embodiment:

```json
{ "item": "queue-version-error", "severity_rank": 2, "category": "queue",
  "owner": "queue-agent", "auto_actionable": true,
  "remediation": { "suggested_action": "queue-drain",
    "doer": "worker-B", "trigger": "on_fire",
    "closing_probe": "SELECT COUNT(*) FROM version_queue WHERE status='error'",
    "pass": "= 0", "max_attempts": 3, "backoff_h": [1,4,24],
    "deadline_h": 72 } }
```

[0319] At step 2510 a remediation consumer picks items in severity order, invokes the doer and books exactly one outcome bucket per scanned item. At step 2512 the closing probe is run; on pass the item closes at step 2514 with the evidence row, a prediction scored if present, and the register updated (`last_fired`, hit count). On fail the item is retried at the next backoff step up to `max_attempts`, after which it becomes `needs_human` and is routed through the interrupt gate of section 9 only if interrupt-eligible, otherwise reassigned to another doer.

### 41.4 Loop breaker

[0320] Status: built and operating as a signature rule (3 repeats in 24 hours files a root-cause item; the kernel map lists hourly remediation-consumer steps including the loop breaker); the audited deployer lacked an equivalent for retries.

[0321] At step 2516 the breaker counts attempts per (target, error signature). When the count reaches k (for example 3) the target's retry is suspended, a root-cause item is filed to the target's program, and a half-open probe is scheduled at a longer interval (for example 24 hours); a pass closes the breaker and a fail extends the interval. Worked example, counterfactual and computed from audit counts: the deploy ledger had 76 rows, 54 failed (54 / 76 = 71 percent), of which 26 (`plane-b-assistant`, each hour a downgrade from `v1.1.0` to `1.0.0` with error code 10021) and 25 (`worker-D`, each hour a syntax error against a corrupt canonical file) came from two targets, 51 of 76 rows (67 percent) and 51 of 54 failures (94 percent). A breaker with k = 3 would have permitted 3 failed attempts per target; 26 - 3 = 23 and 25 - 3 = 22, so 45 of the 51 repeated attempts (88 percent) would not have been made. A further class was found in a self-heal table: 33 hourly occurrences each for six `drift`, five `health-ver` and four `version-format` targets, not covered by any open item.

[0322] Alternatives: backoff doubling from 1 hour to a cap of 24 hours (attempts at hours 0, 1, 3, 7, 15, 31, 55); a breaker re-armed when the target's source hash changes; a breaker that routes to a different doer after k failures.

## 42. Silent-drop reconciliation, integration defects and deploy subsystem audit (FIG. 25)

### 42.1 Silent-drop reconciliation

[0323] Status: built, partially operating in the reference implementation.

[0324] At step 2518 each consuming loop reports, per run, the number of items scanned and the number placed in each outcome bucket, and the reconciler tests the invariant `scanned == executed + failed + needs_human + no_action`. In pseudocode:

```
for loop in loops:
    r = last_run(loop)
    accounted = r.executed + r.failed + r.needs_human + r.no_action
    if accounted != r.scanned:
        gap = r.scanned - accounted
        open_incident('silent-drop', loop, gap, evidence=r)      # step 2520
        reopen(items_scanned_without_disposition(r))
        block_close(parent_items_of(r))
```

[0325] In the audited run `scanned = 25` and the four buckets summed to 0, so `gap = 25`. Disposition at step 2520 reopens the 25 items and blocks closure of the parent issues, because a tracked signal that was dispatched once, never verified, and marked stale-escalated cannot be called resolved. In the same run `fleet_issue_dispatch` held 34 rows in `queued` with 5 total attempts and no transitions, a state that a second reconciliation across ledgers also detects.

[0326] Technical effect: the reconciliation turns the absence of an executor into a numeric fault (gap greater than 0) that fires on the first run, instead of a loop that reports zero errors while no repairs occur.

### 42.2 Ledger reconciliation (split brain)

[0327] The integration audit found six issue stores (`agent_issues`, `issue_ledger`, `issue_events`, `fleet_issue_log`, `fleet_issue_dispatch`, `fleet_issue_loop`) and 209 tables in one database. Open counts were 12 in the store that operations tools read and 303 in the store that they did not, so tools under-reported open work by 303 / 12 = 25.3 times. A re-measure at 13:28Z gave 303 against 305 an hour earlier, so a rate of plus 150 per day extrapolated from 270 to 305 over 5.5 hours was demoted to an unreplicated estimate, while the 25-fold visibility gap reproduced. The procedure (prophetic as an automatic detector): a reconciliation step unions the stores by a declared key, computes the visibility ratio (open items in all stores divided by open items visible to the operating tools) and fires when the ratio exceeds a threshold (for example 2). Other defects recorded with measured values: a health time series with one row (null score and grade) because a weekly write failed on a missing column; 26 (later 27) finished asynchronous jobs held in `continuing` with non-empty responses and no terminal status, with a staged remedy that writes response and terminal status in one statement, adds `terminal_at`, and reaps rows with a response and no update; and liveness probes covering 10 targets (8 workers and 2 sites, 8 / 55 = 14.5 percent of workers). The document lists withdrawn claims, among them "the self-governing layer does not exist" (inferred from table names; a capability test found 13 ratified gates active). Status: built and operating.

### 42.3 Roster reconciliation

[0328] Step 2522 compares rosters. The audit recorded four lists of what the fleet is: the service registry (55), the deployed census (55), the drift scanner's accumulated set (62 distinct names, a union over time that includes pre-merge names), and a probe-log ghost roster (12 names that failed every probe). A worker that emitted alerts every 15 minutes appeared in none, while the dashboard reported `unregistered: 0` and `ghost: []`, because that check compared the registry with the live script list and a name removed from both is invisible. The reconciliation therefore adds a third, independent source, the set of distinct `alerts.source` and event names observed in the last period, and fires on `emitters - (registry union live list)`. The ghost roster of 12 was matched to a consolidation plan (11 of 12 planned moves); the unmatched one was an undocumented deletion. Status: built and operating for the audit; the emitter-set detector is prophetic (not yet built).

### 42.4 Deploy subsystem audit

[0329] Status: performed in the reference implementation on one occasion; the automatic detector is prophetic.

[0330] The deploy ledger showed 54 of 76 attempts failed (section 41.4). Three defects were separated: (a) a version selector targeting an older version than the current one (a downgrade loop); (b) a corrupt canonical file that is not valid source; (c) a canonical copy importing a module that the repository fix had removed. The deployer's resolver order (section 40.3) explains why a repository fix does not ship: a stale mirror (candidate (a)) wins over `worker.js` (candidate (c)), the drift comparator classifies the worker `deployed-ahead`, and the scan continues forever. Version ordering was worked by hand: `newer("1.14.1","1.14.1-gtd-guard")` finds equal dotted parts, then compares strings, and `"1.14.1" < "1.14.1-gtd-guard"`, so the worker is not ahead and the heal proceeds. A marker written as `const VERSION = '1.1.6-single-module'` may not be read by a parser written for `var VERSION = "..."`; a drift row reporting 1.1.4 for a file declaring 1.1.6 gives two hypotheses with a falsifier (the next scan with the file unchanged).

[0331] The registry's `version` column holds the deployed version, so a registration carrying the canonical version would make canonical equal deployed and suppress the heal; a tombstone at the shadowing mirror path is also needed. Registry lag for one worker (1.2.7 against 1.2.8) was explained as an 87-second gap between a registry refresh (14:01:17Z) and a successful deploy (14:02:44Z) and recorded as not a defect. Four tickets filed by the fleet's detectors (691 to 694) were each classified `CONFIRMED`, `CORROBORATED` or `EXPLAINED`.

[0332] Technical effect: separating three defects and the shadow attributes 51 of 54 failures to two retry targets and identifies one control (a tombstone) that changes which candidate resolves, so that a fix is confirmed by a deciding query and not by a repeated deploy.

## 43. Seed code base at the level of its files (FIG. 26)

[0333] This section describes the seed code base of section 19A file by file, so that a skilled person can rebuild it: the parsing grammar, the arithmetic of each stage, the schema, the SQL of each tick step, the policy validator and the tests. Reference numerals 2602 to 2628 refer to FIG. 26: the runtime 2616 with its scheduled heartbeat 2618, the first scored prediction 2620 and the product claim probes 2622 to 2628 are described in sections 43.4, 44.3 and 46.2. The seed schema is a reduced profile of the records of section 3: `items` corresponds to `agent_issues`, `claims` to `work_claims` (the seed omits the token and fence because one handle writes the table), `predictions` and `outcomes` to `decision_predictions` and `decision_outcomes`, `gate_cards` to `human_actions`, and a `tick_runs` row summarizes the per-step rows of section 15. A gate card carries the identity-bound line as a text prefix `IDENTITY-BOUND:` of its reason field, which is equivalent to the `identity_bound_line` column, and the reason of a card is `why_gated` throughout. "Seed" means a repository of files that, given a tenant configuration, starts an organization. "Tenant configuration" means an idea file 2602, an organization configuration file 2604 and an autonomy policy file 2606. "Portable core" 2608 means the files that are identical for every tenant and are not edited during installation (agent protocol, role files, schema, tick procedure, validator, tests). "Connector" 2610 means a named credential binding for an outside system (a code host, a cloud account, a model API, a chat webhook), referred to by environment variable name. "Org record" 2614 means the files a bootstrap writes about one organization. "Owner step" means a step only the principal can take.

[0334] Status: built; tested offline. In one implementation, on 2026-11-10, a Python suite of 12 tests and a JavaScript suite of 9 tests passed with no network access, and a dry-run bootstrap with no credentials completed with exit code 0. The first provisioning of a real cloud account by the seed has not been performed as of 2026-11-10. The provision and start stages below are implemented in code and exercised only in dry-run mode and in tests.

### 43.1 Files and the idea file grammar

[0335] In one embodiment the seed holds: an idea file (about 0.8 KB); an organization configuration (about 2.3 KB); an autonomy policy (about 8.5 KB); an agent protocol (about 4.8 KB) with one pointer file per agent family and six role files; harness settings for the coding-agent product; a bootstrapper (about 24 KB, standard library only); a server worker (an entry point and the tick, about 12 KB) with a deployment-file template; one schema migration (about 3.5 KB); a continuous-integration workflow; and two test suites. The deployment file and a configuration module for the worker are not stored; the bootstrapper renders them, so the stored seed contains no tenant value.

[0336] The organization configuration 2604 is JSON with keys `runtime` (provider, worker and database names templated on a slug, cron expression default `*/10 * * * *`, runtimes intended later), `roles` (`id`, `count`, `instructions`, `tick_every`), `model_ladder` (tiers with `budget_usd_per_month_min` and aliases for `doer`, `reviewer`, `manager`, plus an alias-to-model map), `connectors` (name to `env`, `required_for`, optional `optional`), `capacity` (`plus_share` 0.2, `max_wip_per_role` 3, `fix_only_cycles_before_rebalance` 3) and `reporting`. Example roles: portfolio-manager count 1, tick_every 6; project-manager `per_outcome`, 1; product-manager `per_outcome`, 3; staff `pool`, 1; reviewer `pool`, 1; operations 1, 1. At a 10-minute base tick, tick_every 6 gives an hourly portfolio manager.

[0337] The idea file is the only file a principal writes. Grammar: optional front matter between `---` lines of `key: value` lines (text after `#` is a comment), then sections introduced by `# <Title>`, lowercased by the parser. Each section yields its bullet lines (beginning `- `) as a list and its other non-empty lines as prose, skipping lines beginning `Example:` or `Each line` (template guidance). Recognised front matter keys: `org_name`, `slug`, `owner`, `owner_contact` (a pointer to a secret store, not an address). Recognised sections: `idea`, `who it is for`, `first outcomes`, `constraints`. The monthly budget is the first match of the case-insensitive expression `budget[^0-9]*([0-9]+(?:\.[0-9]+)?)` in the constraint bullets, else 0. Alternatives: a model parses free text into the same structure, validated against the same schema; a web form renders the same file.

### 43.2 Bootstrapper stages

[0338] The bootstrapper 2612 runs seven stages in fixed order, each inside a guard that (i) records the start time, (ii) calls the stage, (iii) converts any exception into status `blocked` with the exception type and message, (iv) logs stage, status (`ok`, `degraded`, `blocked`), detail, start and finish, and (v) for `blocked` adds a backlog item `BOOT-<STAGE>` (responsible role operations, priority 0, closing probe "boot log stage status ok"). It stops early only if the read stage is blocked before an idea has been parsed. A finalizer rewrites the backlog and seed SQL so that items created after planning are also seeded. The exit code is 0 if no stage is blocked and 2 otherwise, so a continuous-integration system distinguishes a degraded but complete boot from a blocked one. Technical effect: a missing credential or failed command does not silently terminate installation; it becomes a prioritized item with a probe, and later stages still produce a complete, reviewable record. Stages (a) and (b), read and detect, correspond to step 1504 of FIG. 15.

[0339] (a) Read: load the three files; derive organization name, slug (lowercase, non-alphanumeric runs to `-`, at most 40 characters, default `org`), outcomes and budget; run the validator of 43.5. A missing idea file returns `degraded`, boots with an empty charter and adds gate card `GATE-IDEA` (probe `item_done:KIT-0`); an invalid policy returns `blocked` listing each violation. (b) Detect: for each connector test only whether its environment variable is set, never its value; test whether a version-control client, a runtime, a package runner and the platform tool exist on the path; for each missing non-optional connector add a gate card (`why_gated` "a credential only the principal can mint", default "those capabilities stay in dry-run", probe `env:<NAME>`) and return `degraded`. Technical effect: secret values never enter logs or output files (a test plants a value and searches every output), while the principal's remaining work is enumerated as cards.

[0340] (c) Select: sort tiers by minimum budget and take the last whose minimum does not exceed the budget. With tiers at 0, 50 and 500, a budget of 0 or 49.99 selects small models for all three roles; 50 selects medium doer and manager and small reviewer; 500 selects large doer and manager and medium reviewer. If the budget exceeds 0, add gate card `GATE-BUDGET` (spending is identity-bound; default "free tiers only"; probe `manual`). (d) Plan: a role with count `per_outcome` yields one instance per outcome (project `OUT-<i>`); `pool` yields 1 instance below a budget of 50 and 2 otherwise; a number yields that many. Roles whose id ends in `manager` take the manager model, the reviewer the reviewer model, others the doer model. For k outcomes the instance count is 2k + 6 at a budget of at least 50 (portfolio 1, project managers k, product managers k, staff 2, reviewers 2, operations 1) and 2k + 4 below it. The kit backlog of FIG. 15 is created with priority 10 + n: KIT-0 charter (portfolio manager, no trigger); KIT-1 repositories and protection, and KIT-2 runtime provisioned (operations, trigger KIT-0); KIT-3 heartbeat, done check `tick_runs_completed_1h >= 6` (operations, trigger KIT-2); KIT-4 reports reach the principal, and KIT-5 first plus accepted (trigger KIT-3). A constraint containing "nothing public" adds KIT-6 "hold the disclosure gate" at priority 5, outranking the kit. Each outcome becomes item `OUT-<i>` (priority 30 + i, product manager, trigger KIT-0, done check "a roadmap of at most five items, each with goal, user and success metric"). One prediction is created: `P-KIT-3`, metric `tick_runs_completed_1h`, operator `>=`, target 6, due 2 hours after seeding.

[0341] (e) Write: output the charter, roles, backlog and gate cards as JSON, the seed SQL, the deployment file (template placeholders replaced) and a worker configuration module (slug, claim time-to-live 120 minutes, capacity values, and the trust, ladder and surfaces sections of the policy). The seed SQL is only `INSERT OR IGNORE` statements (single quotes doubled), including one `trust_ledger` row per (surface, action class) at the surface's start rung with the baseline reliability 0.5 and zero outcomes, and a `doctrine_lineage` row; running it twice changes nothing (tested). (f) Provision: form four commands (create database, apply migrations, execute seed file, deploy). Dry run records them and returns `degraded`. Apply mode returns `blocked` unless the cloud token and account identifier are present and the package runner exists; otherwise it lists databases, reuses one of the same name or creates one and parses its identifier, substitutes it in the deployment file and runs the rest, raising an exception carrying the last 400 characters of error output on any non-zero exit. (g) Start: record that the schedule is armed and that KIT-3 closes only when six complete ticks are observed in an hour. A four-line report and the boot log are written last.

### 43.3 Schema

[0342] The additive migration holds (default timestamps omitted):

| Table | Columns |
|---|---|
| org | key PK, value |
| roles | id PK, role, project, tick_every, model, last_tick_at |
| items | id PK, title, kind (work, plus, fix, amendment, ladder), project, owner_role, trigger_on, done_check, closing_probe, priority (0 highest), status (open, ready, claimed, done, blocked, superseded, cancelled), disposition, blocker, surface, action_class, created_at, updated_at |
| claims | item_id PK references items, agent, claimed_at, heartbeat_at |
| gate_cards | id PK, what, why_gated, default_in_effect, prepared_by_fleet, closing_probe, status, closed_at |
| predictions | id PK, item_id, metric, op, target REAL, due_at, observed, verdict (hit, miss, null) |
| outcomes | id PK, item_id, agent, surface, action_class, result (verified, failed, hidden_failure, silent_drop), evidence, at |
| trust_ledger | surface, action_class, rung, reliability, outcomes, updated_at; PK (surface, action_class) |
| dispatches | id PK, tick_id, role_id, item_id, model, status, at |
| tick_runs | id PK, started_at, finished_at, steps_completed, steps_total, report, errors |
| doctrine_lineage | version PK, source, adopted_at |

[0343] The primary key of `claims` on `item_id` permits at most one live claim per item, so a second claim insert fails. An index on `items(status, priority)` serves dispatch. Migration policy: additive only; a later migration drops data only after export to an object store. In this seed schema the `tick_runs` table is the seed's tick record, one row per tick with the columns shown, whereas the `tick_runs` table of section 3 holds one row per step; the seed's `trust_ledger` (rung, reliability, outcomes, updated_at) is the windowed profile T3 of the trust ledger of section 3 (see the trust computations after paragraph [0060]); and the seed has no `failure_modes` table.

### 43.4 The seven-step tick

[0344] `runTick(db, {config, env, now})` runs over a handle exposing `prepare(sql).bind(...).all()/first()/run()`, the contract of an edge relational database; the same function runs in the worker and, in tests, over an in-memory SQLite shim. It inserts a `tick_runs` row with `steps_total` 7, then runs seven steps in order, each in try/catch.
