A failing step appends to the error list and creates item `TICKFAIL-<step>-<hour>` (priority 0, responsible role operations) through an `ensureItem` helper that inserts only a new identifier; the creation is itself guarded because the database may be what failed. Technical effect: with a table dropped in a test, six steps completed and exactly one deduplicated repair item existed.

[0345] Step 1, reconcile: `SELECT id FROM items WHERE status IN ('done','superseded','cancelled') AND (disposition IS NULL OR disposition='')`; each result is set to `open` and an `outcomes` row (surface `work_tracker`, action `create_update_close`, result `silent_drop`) is inserted; then `UPDATE items SET status='ready' WHERE status='open' AND (trigger_on IS NULL OR trigger_on IN (SELECT id FROM items WHERE status='done'))`. Step 2, reap: claims whose `heartbeat_at` is older than now minus 120 minutes are deleted and their `claimed` items return to `ready`. Step 3, probe gates: split each open card's probe at the first colon; `env` is satisfied if the secret binding is present in the presence map (the worker passes booleans only), `item_done` if the item is done, `manual` never by the tick; a satisfied card is closed with a time. Step 4, score: predictions with null verdict and `due_at` not after now are evaluated with a table of built-in metric functions (`tick_runs_completed_1h`, `items_done`, `open_gate_cards`, `silent_drops_7d`) and operators `>= <= > < =`; the verdict is `hit` if observed op target holds, else `miss`; an unknown metric or operator scores `miss`, so a prediction cannot stay unscored by naming a metric that does not exist.

[0346] Step 5, accrue trust: group outcomes by (surface, action class) into n, successes (`verified`) and severe (`hidden_failure` or `silent_drop`). Reliability = (successes + 0.5 x 2) / (n + 2). Worked: 10 of 10 gives 11/12 = 0.917; 9 of 10 gives 10/12 = 0.833; 8 of 10 gives 9/12 = 0.75; 3 of 8 gives 4/10 = 0.40. The ledger row is upserted but its rung is not changed. Promotion is indicated if the rung index is below the ceiling index of the surface's `max_permission` (none to observed, propose to assisted, execute to self-extending), n is at least 10, reliability is at least 0.8 and severe is 0; demotion if the rung index is above 0 and (reliability is below 0.6 or severe is above 0). An indication opens one item `LADDER-<surface>-<action>-<target rung>` (kind `ladder`, responsible role operations, priority 0 for demotion and 20 for promotion, done check "policy pull request merged after review by a different agent"). Technical effect: scope changes only through a reviewed change to a data file; in a test, 12 verified outcomes on a money action opened a promotion to assisted, the ceiling of a propose surface, and the ledger rung stayed unchanged.

[0347] Step 6, dispatch: for each role with `tickId % tick_every = 0` and fewer than 3 claims, select `SELECT id FROM items WHERE status='ready' AND owner_role=? AND (project IS NULL OR project=? OR ? IS NULL) AND id NOT IN (SELECT item_id FROM claims) ORDER BY priority, created_at LIMIT 1`, insert a claim, set the item `claimed`, record a `dispatches` row and update `last_tick_at`. Step 7, plus share: over the last 20 done items, if at least 10 exist and the share of kind `plus` is below half of `plus_share` (below 0.10), open `REBALANCE-<date>` (portfolio manager, priority 5); for example 1 plus in 20 is 0.05 and opens it. The tick ends by writing a four-line report (what happened, what was decided, what is next, what is open) to `tick_runs`. A health route returns the latest row with status 200 if `steps_completed = steps_total` and 503 otherwise, so an external monitor can use it as a heartbeat probe.

### 43.5 Policy file, validator, protocol, tests

[0348] The policy file 2606 holds `interrupt_gate` (rule, the five card fields what, why_gated, default_in_effect, prepared_by_fleet, closing_probe, examples), `standing_limits` (six: harness permission checks, classifier refusals, the secret lock, outreach consent and opt-out, the principal's override, another session's live claim), `trust` (baseline 0.5, prior weight 2, idle decay 0.01 per decay period (a tick in section 24.3, a day in the seed), and a list of what trust never reaches: harness permissions, secrets, the gate, standing limits), `ladder` (seven rungs with meanings, promote_when min_outcomes 10, min_reliability 0.8, no_hidden_failure_days 14; demote_when reliability below 0.6 or any hidden failure, silent drop or gate bypass; the ceiling map) and `surfaces`. In one embodiment nine surfaces carry 26 action classes: repository 6, runtime 5, work tracker 2, internal chat 1, outreach 3, DNS 3, money 2, legal 2, secrets 2. Each surface has `max_permission` and `start_rung`; each action has `reversible`, `identity_bound` and optionally `reversible_path`, `denied`, `reason`, `harness_deny` (shell-command patterns). Example start rungs: repository delegated; runtime and DNS assisted; work tracker and internal chat autonomous; outreach, money, legal, secrets observed with ceiling propose.

[0349] The validator returns problems: a start rung not among the rungs; a maximum permission not in none, propose, execute; a start rung above its ceiling; an irreversible and identity-bound action that carries a reversible path (then it would not be gated); an irreversible, non-identity-bound action with neither a reversible path nor `denied`; standing limits lacking entries naming the harness, the secret lock and the override. Examples: force push to the default branch is irreversible, not identity-bound, denied; a destructive migration carries the path "export the table to the object store first"; spending is gated. A test sets the money start rung to autonomous (ceiling for propose is assisted) and expects a problem. A mirror test requires every `harness_deny` pattern of gated repository, runtime and secrets actions (for example `Bash(git push --force:*)`, `Bash(gh repo delete:*)`, `Bash(wrangler d1 delete:*)`, `Bash(wrangler secret put:*)`) to appear in the harness settings' deny list, which has 18 rules and sits beside 10 broad allow entries; other gated actions rely on the gate card and, for outreach, money, legal and secrets, on a ceiling of propose. Alternative: generate the harness settings from the policy.

[0350] The protocol file has a managed block with version comments, replaced alone by a synchronizer, and eight parts: unchanging rules (record in code, no secret values, accuracy, never swallow an error); the tick; act, verify, report (including "a refusal is an input": record it and take another path, never re-route the same action past the same check); claims and handoff (branch `agent/<item>-<slug>`, draft pull request within minutes); item format; review (never one's own work, at least one plus); plussing; changing the rules. Each role file lists what the role owns, its tick, its steps and a "never" list. Pointer files for two agent families import the protocol.

[0351] The workflow has job `test` (Python tests, dry-run bootstrap, JavaScript tests) and job `apply` (needs `test`; manual or default-branch push; cloud secrets from the host's secret store; apply-mode bootstrap; commit the org record). A boot without secrets exits 2 and fails the job visibly. The Python tests cover: policy validity; the mirror; money, legal, secrets and outreach never reaching `execute`; a bad policy caught; a credential-free dry run writing all files with every card having a probe and default; outcomes becoming project managers; done checks on all items; the disclosure item; idempotence; no planted secret in any output; a missing idea degrading; budget selecting the tier; apply without credentials giving exit 2 with `BOOT-PROVISION` in backlog and seed SQL. The JavaScript tests cover: all seven steps with priority-ordered dispatch and a trigger holding back a dependent; one dropped table; silent-drop reopening; stale-claim release after 3 hours; gate closure on an `env` probe; a hit verdict; ceiling-bounded promotion; hidden-failure demotion; and reliability 0.5 at zero outcomes.

### 43.6 Limits of the seed as built

[0352] Not implemented as of 2026-11-10 and described as embodiments to build: (i) the tick writes a claim and a dispatch row, but no component yet calls a model with the role file and item and writes outcomes back, so no seed item reaches `done` by itself, and `no_hidden_failure_days` and idle decay are carried in the policy but not evaluated; (ii) severe outcomes are counted over all time, so one silent drop on a pair indicates its demotion permanently; a 14-day window is an alternative; (iii) a ladder item identifier has no date, so a repeated promotion to the same rung would not reopen the item; (iv) at the lowest model tier all aliases coincide, so reviewer and doer share a model family unless a second provider is configured; (v) repository creation (KIT-1) awaits a code-host credential and is a gate card.

## 44. Lessons as design rules, the product boundary and product claims as live probes

### 44.1 Lessons of a prior self-booting system

[0353] The seed derives from an earlier experiment in booting a multi-agent system from physical media (30 commits, 2026-08-11 to 2026-11-09), which did not reach its goal but whose boot tests yielded rules. Status: built as code with tests in the reference implementation.

| Rule | Mechanism in the seed | Technical effect |
|---|---|---|
| The boot never exits | Stage guard and per-step guard turn faults into items | One fault cannot stop the heartbeat |
| Test the whole chain early (nine defects found only by emulated boots) | Credential-free dry run in CI on every push; KIT-2 and KIT-3 need a real deploy and six ticks | Integration defects surface before a cloud account is touched |
| Never swallow an error | Exit codes checked; last 400 characters of error output kept | Failures carry their cause into the backlog |
| Verify before depending (a nonexistent package, a wrong filename) | Stage (b) checks tools and inputs and logs them | No stage starts on a false assumption |
| Capability from the environment | Tier from budget; start rungs from policy | The principal writes one file |
| Safety is a classification | `reversible` and `identity_bound` flags, validator, mirrored deny list | The gate is testable data |
| Local first, then widen | Low start rungs; widening by reviewed change only | Scope follows measured outcomes |
| Unfinished work is a lesson | Nothing is done without a closing probe | An unobserved done check stays open |
| Instructions are part of the artifact | Protocol, pointers, role files from the first commit | A cold agent needs no chat history |

### 44.2 Product boundary file and check

[0354] To make a self-governing system separable into a product and its first customer, one embodiment keeps a boundary file classifying every deployed worker as `core` (the product), `app` (an application on it) or `plane` (a separated plane that never ships). In one implementation the file, dated 2026-11-10, lists 40 workers (20 core, 12 app, 8 plane), 10 core scripts (canary revert, deploy probes, review gate, migration applier, boundary check and others), 7 core workflows and 22 core tables (including the issue ledger, work claims, trust ledger, decision log, decision predictions and outcomes, failure modes, blocker red-teams, doctrine proposals and tick runs). A worker may carry an `entangled` note where core and application share a file; two core workers carry one. Status: built and operating in the reference implementation.

[0355] The check: (1) enumerate live workers, meaning directories of the repository root not beginning `.`, not named `archive`, containing a worker entry file and no `RETIRED` or `FOLDED` marker; (2) add "unclassified live worker" for each live worker absent from the manifest; (3) add "classified worker is not live" for each manifest worker not live; (4) add a violation for an unknown class or missing `role` text; (5) add a violation for each listed script, workflow or document path that does not exist; (6) count per class and list entangled workers; (7) exit 1 if any violation exists. Continuous integration runs it on every change, so a new worker cannot merge without a decision on which side of the product it lies. Technical effect: separability is maintained at the change that would break it, not found by a later audit. Alternatives: classification by directory convention; a fourth class for shared libraries; a dependency graph failing when a core worker imports an app worker (not yet implemented); classification of tables. The manifest records phases: P0 classification and check (done); P1 short default policy pack, P2 core tables in their own database, P3 tenant settings from configuration, P4 a second installation outside the original repository (all open as of 2026-11-10). Section 45 and the non-edge embodiments of section 46 are prophetic embodiments of P2 to P4.

### 44.3 Product claims as live probes

[0356] Each claim a product page makes is a row of `product_claims` (`id`, `product`, `sort`, `claim`, `probe_sql`, `evidence_note`, `created_at`). The probe is one read-only SELECT returning `expected` (the literal `ok`), `observed` (`ok` or a failure phrase) and `detail` (a sentence with counts). A claims page may run each probe when viewed; and a remediation contract of class `claim-ultrastable-<id>` copies the probe, with transport `d1-query`, at most 3 attempts, cadence 1 hour and an escalation target, so a claim that stops being true fails a contract and is escalated like any failure mode. A gateway refuses any probe that is not one SELECT and shows a read failure as its own state. The migration logs its own prediction: pass share of claim verifications at least 0.85 within 48 hours, falsified if a failing claim is neither fixed nor dropped. Status: built and operating in the reference implementation.

| Claim id | Probe summary | Passes when |
|---|---|---|
| own-work | issues with a code-task line created in 7 days | count above 0 |
| predictions-scored | decision outcomes in 7 days (all-time matched over total in detail) | count above 0 |
| tick-running | tick_runs rows in the last 2 hours | count above 0 |
| trust-ledger | latest trust ledger refresh | within 3 hours |
| failure-detectors | active detector contracts of failure-mode classes | count above 0 |
| no-silent-drop | issue accounting view | silent drops and unrecorded deletes both 0 |
| decisions-logged | decision log rows in 7 days | count above 0 |

[0357] A failure shows why the rule exists. In one test run a claims page read "3 of 7 claims pass" with a message that a probe was not a single SELECT: four probes held a semicolon inside a text literal formatting `detail`, and the probe runners refuse any statement containing a semicolon. A follow-up migration replaced the separator with a comma in the table and re-copied the probe into each contract in the same change, predicting zero rows containing a semicolon within 1 hour and 7 of 7 passing within 2 hours. Claims and contracts change together so they cannot drift; a second check fails the build if the claim identifiers in the boundary file and the probe rows disagree. The measured state used as proof in one test run: 6,604 issues through the ledger; 3,880 merged changes since 2026-08-10 (1,245 in 7 days), 624 opened by the code loop and 416 by a hosted coding agent; 1,056 outcomes scored, 771 matched (73 percent); 634 failure modes with detectors; 388 blocker red-teams; 2,176 decisions; 7,590 tick steps. The product document lists its own gaps: one installation and customer, core entangled with applications, 563 open issues against a target of 0, a match rate scored by the system itself, policy written as principal directives. Technical effect: a sales claim is mechanically tied to data, so a claim that ceases to hold is surfaced as an incident rather than found by a reader.

## 45. Tenant isolation, one-command install, and the product as its first tenant

[0358] Status: prophetic (not yet built). This section extends sections 43 and 44 to further tenants; nothing here has been run. The existing installation (customer zero, 44.2) is not an instance of the isolation design.

### 45.1 Tenant isolation

[0359] "Control plane" means the component that provisions tenants and holds the product's records about them. "Tenant plane" means one tenant's database, worker, schedule, secrets and repositories. Isolation levels: level 1, a separate account or project per tenant with the control plane holding a deployment credential scoped to it; level 2 (default, as the seed already creates one database and worker per slug), a separate database and worker in a shared account; level 3, a shared database with a `tenant_id` column on every core table enforced by row-level policy or a view layer adding the predicate. Control-plane schema (illustrative): `tenants(id, slug, level, runtime, region, status)`, `tenant_versions(tenant_id, core_version, policy_version, applied_at)`, `tenant_bindings(tenant_id, connector, env_name, present)`, `tenant_caps(tenant_id, model_usd_month, tick_per_hour)`. The control plane has no read path to tenant items; each tenant's tick pushes an hourly aggregate (ticks, claims, predictions scored and matched, silent drops, cost estimate).

[0360] Isolation probe, per tenant pair (A, B), daily: (1) write canary row `iso-<nonce>` in A through A's handle; (2) with B's credentials attempt to read A's database, object prefix and secrets; (3) expect denial at levels 1 and 2 and zero rows at level 3, where each core query is also run with a foreign `tenant_id`; (4) delete the canary; (5) record `isolation_checks(a, b, surface, expected, observed, at)`. Any mismatch is a severe outcome on surface `tenant_isolation`, opens a priority-0 item and, in one example, disables the pair's schedules after 1 failure. The probe is also a product claim row (`tenant-isolation`) in the form of 44.3, so the property is advertised only while it passes. Secrets and cost: connector names resolve to tenant-unique environment names (`<SLUG>_CLOUD_TOKEN`); a test searches the core for every registered tenant's slug and account identifier and fails on a hit; a tenant's model spend is capped by `tenant_caps` (at 80 percent, illustrative, the tier drops; at 100 percent a gate card opens for the tenant's principal because spending is identity-bound).

### 45.2 One-command install

[0361] The command takes a slug, a runtime (edge, gcp, container) and an idea file path, and runs idempotent steps logged in an installer log: (1) verify preconditions and open gate cards for missing connectors as in stage (b); (2) fetch a signed bundle (a manifest `ultrastable-core.json` of files, SHA-256 digests, core version, migrations) and stop on any digest mismatch; (3) run the bootstrapper of 43.2; (4) call the adapter `provision` (46.1) to create the database, apply additive core migrations and seed; (5) `bind` connectors, falling into gate cards for values the principal has not supplied; (6) `deploy` with the schedule and run probes (the health route returns 503 until the first tick completes); (7) wait for the first tick and print its four-line report; (8) register the tenant and enable isolation and claim probes. Exit codes: 0 all ok; 2 blocked with items written; 3 bundle verification failed. Re-running performs only steps whose probes fail, so the same command upgrades a tenant. Technical effect: human input is reduced to the idea file and owner steps, and an altered core cannot be installed unnoticed. Alternatives: a hosted form that writes the idea file; a template repository whose first push triggers the apply job of 43.5.

### 45.3 The product as tenant number one

[0362] The product's roadmap is written as an idea file and run on the extracted core as the first tenant, so the product improves through the loops it sells. Illustrative file: `org_name: Ultrastable`; idea "a self-governing layer around the coding agents a team already uses"; users "an engineering lead who runs many services; a team that owes auditors an account of what its agents did"; outcomes "a second installation on a repository outside the original runs the core without the application workers" (OUT-1), "a short default policy pack replaces principal directives" (OUT-2), "core tables run from their own database" (OUT-3), "a design partner completes the one-command install" (OUT-4); constraints "no external visibility until the principal says so" (an external-visibility constraint) and a budget chosen by the principal. OUT-1 to OUT-3 correspond to phases P4, P1, P2. Illustrative predictions: OUT-3 "isolation probe pass share >= 0.99 over 7 days after cutover"; OUT-1 "dry-run bootstrap and tick tests pass on the second repository within 1 hour of install"; OUT-4 "install command to first scored prediction <= 3 hours" (the seed's prediction allows 2 hours for six ticks). Owner-only steps (for example registrations in the principal's name) are identity-bound gate cards that the tenant's portfolio manager keeps open without assigning them in prose, while work continues. The tenant's claims run against its own database, so the product is the first subject of its proof; a defect the tenant finds in the core is filed on the same ledger, fixed by the code loop, deployed through the change path and reaches all tenants through the versioned bundle. Status: prophetic; customer zero already runs its own backlog with the same loops in entangled form.

## 46. A cloud-provider embodiment mapped component by component, and a worked example (FIG. 27)

### 46.1 Adapter interface and mapping

[0363] Status: the edge embodiment 2706 is built and operating in one implementation (about 39 scheduled workers, section 21; seed tested offline). The cloud-provider embodiment 2708 and container embodiment 2710 are prophetic (not yet built). The seed's configuration lists a cloud-run runtime as supported later, and the bootstrapping document states that other runtimes reimplement only provision and start. The portable core 2702 (schema, tick steps, ledgers, policy data, protocol, probes) calls no platform API. The adapter interface 2704 has six ports implemented once per platform: schedule 2712 (invokes `runTick` at a cadence); store 2714 (a handle with `prepare().bind().all()/first()/run()`, `meta.changes`, `meta.last_row_id`); secrets 2716 (a presence map, values only to the process that needs them); model 2718 (an alias resolved to an endpoint, returning a completion and token counts); deploy 2720 (versioned release, traffic shift, revert); health 2722 (the last tick report). The seed demonstrates the store port: `runTick` runs on an edge database and on an in-memory SQLite handle.

[0364] Schedule 2712. Edge: a cron trigger calls the scheduled handler. Cloud: a scheduler job posts every 10 minutes to an HTTPS route of a container service with an identity token of a service account holding only the invoker role; the route checks the audience and calls `runTick`. Because a scheduler can deliver twice, an adapter migration adds `slot INTEGER UNIQUE` to `tick_runs`; the handler inserts `now / 600000` as the slot and returns if the insert conflicts. Alternative: a run-to-completion job. Store 2714. Embodiment A, a managed Postgres-class database: a shim translates `?` to `$n`, `INSERT OR IGNORE` to `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING`, `datetime('now')` to `now()` stored as text in `YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM:SS` so the string comparisons of 43.4 hold, `AUTOINCREMENT` to `BIGSERIAL`, and `last_row_id` to `RETURNING id`; the aggregations and the `NOT IN` dispatch query map directly, so this is preferred. Embodiment B, a document database: each table is a collection keyed by primary key; claim uniqueness comes from a create-only write keyed by item identifier; counts come from aggregation queries or transactional counters; dispatch is an indexed query on (status, owner_role, priority) followed by a transactional claim; steps 1, 5 and 6 of 43.4 are rewritten over the document interface and the tests of 43.5 are rerun.

[0365] Secrets 2716. Edge: platform secret bindings, presence only. Cloud: a secret manager, mounted as environment variables at deploy time; the tick builds its presence map from variable names; the tick's service account cannot read payloads other than those mounted, so the secret lock is enforced by identity permissions as well as by code. Model 2718. Edge: an edge inference binding and a hosted-model connector. Cloud: a managed model endpoint addressed by project, region and endpoint identifier, with the alias map extended by endpoint identifiers; a deploy-time check fails if reviewer and doer aliases resolve to one family when two are available. Deploy 2720. Edge: versioned deploy, canary, revert (section 12). Cloud: each deploy is a new revision; 10 percent of traffic (illustrative) shifts to it, the probe set runs against the revision's tagged URL, and traffic reaches 100 percent only if probes that passed before still pass, else returns to the prior revision, preserving "passed before and fails after reverts". Health 2722. Edge: a route; cloud: the route plus an uptime check each minute raising a card on failure. Remaining mappings: workflows authenticate through workload identity federation, leaving no long-lived key; executor adapters stay coordinated through the claim ledger; provider budget alerts feed the `money` surface, whose ceiling stays propose. Container embodiment 2710: the same image under any orchestrator; a CronJob calls the route; the shim of embodiment A; orchestrator secrets; any inference endpoint; image-tag rollout and rollback; liveness probes; a single-machine variant uses the operating system's timer and an embedded database file. Technical effect: moving between embodiments changes six adapters and no tick step, policy, ledger or protocol, and the tests of 43.5 are the conformance suite: an adapter passes if the JavaScript tests pass over its store handle and the bootstrap tests pass with its provision stage replaced.

### 46.2 Worked example from idea file to first scored prediction

[0366] Status: the files and numbers below were produced on 2026-11-10 by running the seed's bootstrapper in dry-run mode, loading the generated seed SQL into an in-memory SQLite database, and running the seed's tick 14 times with simulated time advancing 10 minutes per tick. This is an offline simulation of the built seed; no cloud account was provisioned. Two shortcuts: the database and schedule came from the test harness, and item KIT-2 was set done by direct update, with a disposition, before tick 2 in place of the provision command. The idea is illustrative. Step 1, the idea file:

```
---
org_name: Tool Share
slug: tool-share
owner: Principal
owner_contact: set-in-secret-store
---
# Idea
A neighbourhood tool library where people borrow tools instead of buying them, booked from a phone.
# Who it is for
- Renters and new homeowners who need a tool once or twice a year
# First outcomes
- A landing page that explains the idea and collects interest
- A booking prototype that ten neighbours try
# Constraints
- Budget per month: 50 USD
- Nothing external until the principal says so
```

[0367] Step 2, dry-run bootstrap with no credentials. Report: "Happened: seed 0.1.0 booted 'Tool Share' (dry run): 4/7 stages ok. Decided: 10 role instances on models {doer: medium, reviewer: small, manager: medium}. Next: run with --apply once the gate cards below are closed. Open: 9 backlog item(s), 5 gate card(s)." Read, select, plan and write are `ok`; detect, provision and start are `degraded`; exit code 0. Arithmetic: budget 50 selects the second tier; 2 outcomes give 2 x 2 + 6 = 10 instances. The backlog has 9 items (KIT-0 to KIT-6 and OUT-1, OUT-2). The five cards are four secrets (`env:<NAME>` probes) and `GATE-BUDGET` (probe `manual`), for example:

```
{"id": "GATE-SECRET-CLOUD_API_TOKEN",
 "what": "Set the CLOUD_API_TOKEN secret (connector 'cloudflare', needed for runtime, dns)",
 "why_gated": "a credential only the owner can mint",
 "default_in_effect": "runtime, dns stay in dry-run",
 "prepared_by_fleet": "the binding name is already in config; only the value is missing",
 "closing_probe": "env:CLOUD_API_TOKEN"}
```

[0368] Step 3, files written: charter, roles, backlog, gate cards, seed SQL, boot log, boot report, a deployment file (worker and database `tool-share-ultrastable`, cron `*/10 * * * *`) and a worker configuration module. The seed SQL has 4 org rows, 10 roles rows, 9 items rows, 5 gate cards, 26 trust ledger rows, 1 prediction and 1 lineage row, for example `INSERT OR IGNORE INTO predictions (id, item_id, metric, op, target, due_at) VALUES ('P-KIT-3', 'KIT-3', 'tick_runs_completed_1h', '>=', 6, datetime('now', '+2 hours'));`.

[0369] Step 4, first ticks. Tick 1 completed 7/7 steps; reconcile made KIT-0 and KIT-6 ready (no trigger) and dispatched nothing, since no one-tick role owns a ready item. After KIT-2 was marked done, tick 2 made KIT-3 ready and dispatched it to operations-1. At tick 6 the portfolio manager is due (6 divides 6) and received KIT-6 (priority 5) before KIT-0 (priority 10), which it received at tick 12. No other item reached done, because the seed has no executor (limit (i) of 43.6). Step 5, the first scored prediction: `P-KIT-3` is due 2 hours after seeding, reached at the 13th tick (12 intervals after the first). Step 4 of that tick counted ticks with all 7 steps complete finished within the last hour, which was 6, and reported "prediction P-KIT-3: tick_runs_completed_1h=6 hit". The stored row: observed 6, verdict hit. Because the target 6 equals the number of 10-minute intervals in an hour, there is no margin: one missed or incomplete tick gives 5 and a miss, the intended sensitivity of a heartbeat probe.

[0370] Step 6, continuation (prophetic; needs the executor not yet built). A staff role claims an item, logs a prediction of the form `<metric> <op> <number> within <N> h`, opens a draft pull request on `agent/<item>-<slug>`; a reviewer of a different model reproduces the result by another path and records an `outcomes` row `verified`. After 10 verified repository merges with no severe outcome, reliability is 11/12 = 0.917 and the tick opens `LADDER-repo-merge_pr_green-autonomous`; a reviewed policy change moves the rung. When the principal sets the secret of a gate card, step 3 of a later tick observes the binding and closes the card without a message. Step 7, another runtime: with the mapping of 46.1, steps 1 to 3 are identical; provision creates a managed database, a container service and a scheduler job, the store shim translates the seed SQL, and the first scheduler call on the 10-minute boundary produces tick 1 with its slot value; steps 5 and 6 are unchanged. Installation is complete when the health route returns 200 and the prediction has a verdict.

## 47. Owner console and one-view status

[0371] "Owner console" means a network-served user interface, operated by a principal, that presents the status of every program, project, product and agent of an organization in one view, and through which the principal issues commands and answers questions. "Console host" means the edge worker that serves the owner console and its programmatic interface. "Owner card" means a row in the principal queue (section 14) describing one step only the principal can take. "Tap" means one selection by the principal of a pre-defined button, by touch or click, requiring no typed text. "Credential" means the kind of authority presented with a request: an owner session, a machine loop token or a legacy key. "Step-up" means a requirement that the owner session have been verified by a fresh emailed code within a window. These terms are used identically below. Sections 47 to 50 describe the console of the reference fleet; section 51 describes an instance whose dashboard is prophetic as to the unconfirmed features.

[0372] Technical problem. Status spread over an infrastructure console, an issue tracker, chat transcripts and email cannot be read at a glance, a principal is asked to do work agents could do, and an owner-only control is easy to expose or to automate by mistake. The console collects status into one single-page view on the organization's own infrastructure, shows the principal only items that need the principal, and routes every state-changing action through authenticated, audited, allow-listed routes that record which credential acted.

### 47.1 One view of every program, product and agent (built and operating)

[0373] In one embodiment the console host is a single edge worker (about 1.5 MB of deployed code) with relational-database bindings, service bindings to other fleet workers, a scheduled trigger every 5 minutes (a standby heartbeat; the full state refresh runs on quarter-hour runs) and a code map in which each functional area (cost, research, reach, autonomy, reliability, platform) has banner-marked regions that a test requires to be registered. The home page "/" is three screens on a static shell that paints at once: Now, Readers and Code. Separate small reads `GET /api/v3/now` and `GET /api/v3/readers` carry their own age; `POST /api/v3/code` starts a command-line run and `GET /api/v3/code/<id>` returns its steps. An older overview remains at `/classic`, and each area has a page and a feed (`GET /api/area/<id>`, `GET /api/areas`). In one embodiment the console opens the Now screen with four numbers tied to the principal's goals (readers, fleet cost, owner load, reliability) and drops a count of off-target metrics because nothing can be done with it. Status: built and operating in the reference implementation.

[0374] Each read declares its source, lag, authentication and cache in an index row (for example `GET /api/status`, cache "shared-cache 15s, ETag", `GET /api/triage` lag 15 s, `/api/owner/*` "private no-store"), a content-based ETag excludes time-derived fields so that a conditional read returns 304, and a read with `?since=<revision>` returns `{not_modified:true}`. In one embodiment reads may be served without credentials while state-changing controls require an owner credential. A health read returns capabilities and a list of stated limitations. Technical effect: a status client can poll cheaply without false change.

[0375] Multi-surface view. The same state is exposed on a static home page, a classic overview, per-area pages, a status document, a changes feed (`GET /api/changes`: deploys by worker, issues closed and opened, code-loop merges and new owner cards since a time the page passes from the principal's last visit, clamped to 7 days, titles cut to 90 characters), a managers page (`/managers`, `/api/managers`, showing what the portfolio manager and the five program managers decided each hour, the quality index per program with its 24-hour change, and recurring issue families), and a realtime feed (`/api/live`, schema `fleet-live/v3`) whose revision changes when the owner-queue count changes. A "credit runway" is balance divided by a 30-day average daily spend (shown on a key figure only, never as a card). Status: built and operating in the reference implementation.

[0376] An operations variant of the console serves a page refreshed on a short interval from a state route and a refresh route, and shows the fleet census of section 29.3 with its stated gaps. Status: built and operating in the reference implementation.

### 47.2 Watchmaker index, triage, value and changelog views (built and operating)

[0377] "Watchmaker index" means the count of recurring operations in the organization that still need a person or an interactive session. A table `WATCHMAKER_OPS` lists each recurring operation with a runner, a cadence in hours and a SQL query proving its last run; an operation counts when a person or session runs it, and one whose measurement is missing is listed as unmeasured. A daily run writes a row to `watchmaker_runs`, sets the metric `watchmaker_index` in `metric_registry` and serves `GET /api/watchmaker`. A failure story: a day whose stored measurement made 41 of 68 operations "unmeasured", each counted as needing a person, raised the index to 42 for 16 hours, and the counting rule was corrected. The breach doctor tick (every 10 minutes) is added to the list so that a stalled doctor is visible.

[0378] Triage: `GET /api/triage` answers in one read which root cause each breaching metric belongs to (from the view `v_breach_by_cause`), which breaching metrics map to no cause (named, never counted as zero) and which failure modes recur, and `GET /triage` is a plain-language guide. Value view: the headline is yield per $100, defined as outputs of the last 7 days per $100 of all billing in the same days at list price, shown beside quality; the rule text states "a change counts only if yield per $100 rose, quality did not fall, and spend stayed within the monthly budget; spending nothing is not a win". Each unit has a push lever (raise outputs) and a trim lever (cut spend that buys nothing). Worked example (illustrative): 12 outputs on $8 of spend in the window gives yield 100 x 12 / 8 = 150 per $100. Every subject on the page has an entry stating what it means, why it is shown, what the fleet does by itself and who does it, and `POST /api/owner/act {subject, directive?}` files a ticket built from that entry, never from client text (directive at most 600 characters). A changelog feed (`/api/changelog`, `changelogDigest`) and an architecture view are generated from the release notes and the route index. Status: built and operating in the reference implementation.

### 47.3 Emergency AI control (built, partially operating)

[0379] A separate, smaller console variant places a pause control above its chat, backed by a control authority with a revision counter. Page rules: the mode label is "AI paused", "AI enabled" or "AI status unknown"; unknown applies when no authority confirmation exists, when the last is 15,000 ms old or older, when a mutation is in flight or on any error; status refreshes every 5 seconds and on visibility change. The pause (`POST /api/ai/command {"command":"SHUTDOWN"}`, exact case-sensitive match, or `PUT /api/ai/control {"paused":true}`) needs no prior status read. Resume needs `confirmResume:true` and `expectedRevision`; the authority applies a compare-and-set on the revision (409 on mismatch), and a repeated pause increments the revision. Before every assistant entry, model call and tool dispatch (at most 8 loop iterations) a fresh `GET /ai-control/check` goes through a service binding whose caller identity is a trusted binding property rather than a header; any state other than a valid not-paused 200 returns 503. The design states that this is a dispatch failsafe, not an account-wide spend cap, that a pause may commit between a check and a dispatch, and that fleet-wide coverage depends on every AI path calling the authority. Status: built, partially operating in the reference implementation.

## 48. Owner authentication, session handling and command execution (FIG. 28)

### 48.1 Email-me-a-code sign-in (built and operating)

[0380] "One-time code" means a 6-digit decimal code. At step 2802 the principal types `login` into the command line or taps "Email me a code", which calls the code-request route. At step 2804 the console host runs `cmdSendCode`: it counts rows of `owner_codes` created in the last hour and refuses with status 429 at 6 or more (`CMD_CODE_MAX_PER_HOUR = 6`); if a code was created less than 60 s ago (`CMD_CODE_MIN_GAP_MS`) it sends nothing and tells the principal to check the masked inbox; if the email binding is absent it returns 503; otherwise it draws a code as `r mod 10^6` of a 32-bit cryptographic random value, inserts a row with `expires_ms = now + 10 minutes` (`CMD_CODE_TTL_MS`), stores `code_hash = SHA-256(code | id | salt)` where the salt derives from a server secret, and sends the code by email to one preconfigured owner address held in the worker (a constant, never taken from the request), from a fixed sender. The address is shown only masked (first two characters and the domain). The send outcome is stored in `owner_codes.sent_ok`, and a failed send returns 502 with the reason. The table `owner_codes` has columns `id, code_hash, created_ms, expires_ms, tries, used_ms, visitor, sent_ok`.

[0381] At step 2806 the principal submits the code to `POST /api/cmd/verify`. `cmdVerify` strips non-digits and requires 6 digits (400); refuses with 429 when the sum of `tries` over the last hour reaches 20 (`CMD_VERIFY_FAILS_PER_HOUR`); reads at most the 3 newest rows that are unused, unexpired and sent; skips a row with `tries >= 5` (`CMD_CODE_MAX_TRIES`); compares hashes in constant time; and on a match marks the row used with a compare on `used_ms IS NULL` so a code works once, even under a race. On a miss it increments `tries` on the newest row and returns 401 ("not right" or "no code is waiting"). At step 2808 a success revokes the previous session, generates a random 256-bit token, stores only its SHA-256 in `owner_sessions (token_hash, created_ms, expires_ms, verified_ms, revoked, visitor)`, and sets a cookie `fleet_cmd` with HttpOnly, Secure and SameSite=Lax and a 12-hour lifetime (`CMD_SESSION_MS`). The reply states "Signed in for 12 hours. Destructive actions ask for a fresh code after 15 minutes."

[0382] Worked example. One guess can match any of at most 3 candidate codes compared per attempt, so a guess succeeds with probability at most 3 x 10^-6; the global limit of 20 recorded wrong tries per hour caps the number of guesses at 20 regardless of how many codes exist, which bounds a hostile hour to about 20 x 3 x 10^-6 = 6 x 10^-5. The modulo bias of drawing `r mod 10^6` from a 32-bit value is negligible (2^32 mod 10^6 = 967,296), and rejection sampling may be used instead. Technical effect: an owner-only control is opened without a stored password, an attacker cannot learn whether any other address is allowed (there is none), and a leaked database row cannot be replayed because only hashes are stored. Alternatives: a signed link; a passkey; an edge identity gate delivering a signed assertion (the small variant of section 47.3 verifies an identity-gate assertion header with RS256, issuer, audience and an owner allow-list on every request, 401/403/503, 5,000 ms key fetch timeout). Status: built and operating in the reference implementation. The edge-gate alternative is built with deployment unverified.

### 48.2 Credentials, step-up and the machine token (built and operating)

[0383] `cmdSession` reads the cookie, looks up the hash with `revoked = 0` and an unexpired `expires_ms`, and returns `stepup = (now - verified_ms) < 15 minutes` (`CMD_STEPUP_MS`). `ownerCredential(owner)` returns `loop-token`, `owner-session` or `owner-key`, and every decision row records it (`human_responses.credential`). Rules: reads and read-only commands are open; actions need the owner session; destructive ones (`done`, `dismiss`, ratify, undo, mutating tools, SQL writes, deploy, merge, sending mail in the principal's name) need step-up, otherwise the route returns 401 with `{need:"stepup"}` and the page prompts for a fresh code in place, sends it at once and repeats the tap on verify. The machine loop token (header `x-loop-token`) can file and clear queue entries for loops, but cannot run console commands, approve or hand work back, and a decision on a card matching a hard marker is refused for the loop token. SQL writes are limited: no DROP, ALTER or REPLACE; UPDATE and DELETE need a WHERE and are first backed up to `console_backups`; the tables for the budget, verifications, claims and sign-in are read-only. Technical effect: an automation holding a machine credential cannot take an authority reserved for the principal, and a stolen cookie more than 15 minutes old cannot change state without a fresh code. Status: built and operating in the reference implementation.

### 48.3 Command registry and execution (FIG. 28, steps 2810 to 2822)

[0384] At step 2810 the principal types into the command line (route `/cmd`, `POST /api/cmd/run`) or taps a button; a button is only a typed command stored in `data-` attributes (`data-dec-op`, `data-dec-args`), so typed and tapped commands take the same path. Every state-changing route requires a header `x-fleet-ui: 1` (400 otherwise). At step 2812 the console tests for an owner session; otherwise it returns 401 with `{need:"code"}`. At step 2814 it tests the verb's registry entry (for example `CMD_OPS = { done: {destructive:true}, dismiss: {destructive:true}, snooze: {destructive:false}, note: {...}, task: {...} }`) and, for a destructive verb, the 15-minute step-up (route to step 2822 on staleness). At step 2820 an AI-affecting command passes through the control authority of section 47.3 before step 2816. At step 2816 the verb executes. The command grammar parses `approve`, `decline`, `diagnose`, `retry` and `resolve` in code, without a model, resolving the target (issue id, `#id`, card key, uppercase keyword, queue number or bare verb) against the queue and the audit database; a verb counts as a command only when it stands alone or names its target, so a sentence that merely contains it goes to the model path. Other verbs include `close <id> <evidence>`, `wontfix`, `sql! [db]`, `call <worker> [GET|POST] <path>`, `dispatch <workflow>`, `deploy`, `merge <pr>`, `closepr`, `rerun <run id>` and `reply <id> <text>`; there is no free shell. Fleet tools are reached through a service binding to an operations worker that authenticates the dashboard by a trusted caller property and offers a catalogue; read-only tools (a fixed map such as `fleet_status`, `ops_issues_list`, `ops_d1_query`) are open to the owner session, and any other tool needs step-up (`POST /api/cmd/tool`, name matching `^[a-z0-9_]{2,40}$`).

[0385] Plain-English input from the signed-in principal becomes a task for an agent, anonymous plain-English answers are capped at 5 a day per visitor and by a global daily cap, and model-callable tools that could delete a DNS record on a sentence are deliberately not ported, so they come back as confirm buttons behind a fresh code. Voice: `POST /api/cmd/transcribe` takes the raw recording (webm, ogg, mp4, wav or mp3, at most 8 MB, 413 beyond), requires the owner session (401 "Voice needs your email code"), calls a speech model in the order `whisper-large-v3-turbo` then `whisper` (the first to answer wins, so a retired model costs one fallback) and returns `{ok, text, model, ms}`; the text enters the same typed line . Step 2818: each command writes a row to `cmd_log (id, ts, text, from_url, kind, status, answer, actions_json, model, error, owner, visitor, done_ms)`, and the Now screen shows when the command line last ran and how it ended or that it never ran.

[0386] Undo. `POST /api/cmd/undo {pr}` requires the owner session and step-up, accepts only a pull request on an agent branch (`CMD_AGENT_BRANCH_RE`), and calls `revertPullRequest`, logging a `cmd_log` row of kind `undo`; `GET /api/cmd/tasks` lists tasks with a one-tap undo . SQL updates and deletes are reversible through `console_backups`. Status for 48.3: built and operating in the reference implementation.

[0387] Extended embodiments (prophetic, not yet built): a command table with a JSON schema and an explicit `undo` verb for every mutating command rather than a revert only for agent pull requests; a command that becomes a work item with a claim carrying an expiry and a fence value as in section 13, so each owner command follows the claim, probe and revert path.

## 49. Principal task queue: owner cards, one-tap options, defaults, delegation (FIG. 29)

### 49.1 Card creation and the identity-bound split (built and operating)

[0388] At step 2902 a step or question arises. At step 2904 the system tests whether a reversible form is constructible in scope; if so step 2906 executes it and records a disposition, and no card is created. Otherwise step 2908 tests for an identity-bound line, meaning a credential only the principal can mint, money, a legal or signing act, or the principal's own accounts. The queue is a table `human_actions` with columns including `slug, title, why, default_in_effect, action, sev, status` (`open`, `resolved`, `dismissed`, `rerouted`), `source, copy_text, resolution, resolved_at, updated_at`; creation uses `INSERT OR IGNORE` on the slug. In one embodiment the console filters the queue: items whose source is in `FLEET_HANDLED_SOURCES = ["code-loop","issue-loop"]` leave the owner count, the banner and the area queues and appear under "The fleet is handling" (step 2910), while every other source, including inbox items and cards, remains an owner item; the realtime digest includes `fleet_handling.count`; an offline test evaluates the filter block of the code in a sandbox. The Now screen turns red only for cards carrying an `IDENTITY-BOUND:` line; owner-lane cards matched by a keyword alone are listed as optional. In one embodiment card titles are shown only to the signed-in principal, and any other visitor sees a placeholder.

[0389] Worked example. A migration created four cards: `product-domain-1` (buy a domain), `product-mark-1`, `code-host-org-1` and `entity-1`. The domain card's `why` begins `IDENTITY-BOUND:` (it spends money and registers in the principal's name), its `default_in_effect` states that the product keeps its working name inside the organization and no domain is bought, and `action` gives the steps; the migration's rollback sets the open cards of that migration to `dismissed`. Status: built and operating in the reference implementation.

### 49.2 One-tap options on a card (built and operating)

[0390] At step 2914 the console shows each owner card with a reading in four parts (what it is, why it is shown, what happens if the principal waits, what to do) and buttons of at least 52 px on a phone. In one embodiment the console gives each card four options: "Fleet: you do it", "Snooze 7 days", "I did it" and "Not needed". Decision cards show Approve and Decline for the whole card, for the undecided rest or for one numbered item, and the tap posts at once with no preview and no second confirm; Done, Snooze 3 and 7 days and Not doing appear on cards that are not decisions. A signed-out visitor sees a single "Email me a code to decide" button and no decision button. `POST /api/owner/respond {key, kind, note?, days?}` validates `key` against `^[A-Za-z0-9:._-]{3,160}$`, `kind` in `done|dismiss|snooze|note`, a `note` of at most 500 characters and `days` of 1 to 90 for snooze; `done` and `dismiss` apply only to queue items with the `ha:` key prefix, update `human_actions` guarded by `status='open'` (resolution text names the credential) and every response inserts into `human_responses (key, kind, note, until, credential)`, so a derived item is snoozed rather than closed. In one embodiment the console arranges that no one-tap option asks for a fresh code: "I did it" asks the fleet to verify and close the card, and "Not needed" parks the card for 90 days, because done and dismiss through `/api/owner/respond` are step-up actions. Snooze and Not needed hide the card at once and the page reloads fresh. Status: built and operating in the reference implementation. Steps 2912 (ranking of cards by severity and nearness of deadline), 2916 (a one-tap fix by the principal) and 2918 (the choice to delegate the card to the fleet) complete the sequence of FIG. 29.

### 49.3 Delegation to the fleet (built and operating)

[0391] Step 2920 is the hand-back: `POST /api/v3/handback {key, mode?}` requires the owner session (401 "Sign in with your emailed code"), validates `key` against `^ha:[A-Za-z0-9._-]{1,120}$`, reads the open card, and builds a ticket text starting `OWNER-HANDBACK <key>:` with the card's title, why and action, a definition of done (do the work with every surface that exists, verify live, close with evidence) and a rule that only an irreversible, identity-bound step may stay with the principal, in which case the fleet reopens the card with an `IDENTITY-BOUND:` line, the exact blocker and a closing probe. In `mode: "verify"` the text instead says the owner reports it done and asks for a live check. The route first claims the card (`UPDATE human_actions SET status='rerouted' ... WHERE slug=? AND status='open'`), so a double tap cannot file two tickets, then files the ticket through `ownerTaskFile` with deduplication on identical text; if filing fails the card is restored to `open` and the principal is told with status 502. The resolution is updated with the issue id and a timestamp. In one embodiment the console exposes a one-tap fleet handoff on every visible breach card and re-audits parked code and issue-loop cards through their owning loop with evidence-only closure.

[0392] Closure. At step 2924 the card closes when a closing probe or the fleet's verification observes the step done in the managed system; step 2926 sets `status`, stores evidence in `resolution`, and records the credential. A card the principal does not touch leaves its default in effect (step 2922), so the fleet is not blocked. Technical effect: in one embodiment the number of items the principal must act on is limited to identity-bound steps, reversible work moves to agents with one tap, and a duplicate or lost hand-back is prevented by a claim before filing.

### 49.4 Prompted-recall questions that change the record (built and operating)

[0393] A second queue, the table `owner_asks (plane, kind, ref, question, cue, options_json, priority, created_at, answer, answer_label, answered_at, answered_via)`, holds questions with two to four options plus "Not sure"; the `cue` states evidence so the principal need only recognize an answer. A tap sets `answer`. An `AFTER UPDATE OF answer` trigger `owner_asks_product_profile_au` fires for a fixed list of kinds when the answer is not null or `unsure` and changed, and writes the mapped value by `INSERT OR REPLACE` into `product_profile (product, key, value, source, updated_at)`, which a page served by the console reads (defaults apply if unreadable), so one tap changes that page without a deployment. For delegation, a view `v_product_answers_unfiled` lists answered asks with no issue titled for that answer, and a sweep, specified by a roadmap issue with `code-task` and `code-probe` lines, files one issue per row. A first version used a trigger on `agent_issues` and failed to apply because it exceeded the database's trigger depth ; the view and sweep replaced it. Migration headers record a prediction with a falsifier, apply-by, rollback and a single-point-of-failure review. Status: built and operating in the reference implementation; the sweep is built, partially operating (open roadmap issue).

## 50. Management practice, reports, digests and handoff

### 50.1 Manager and portfolio registers and the compliance checker (built and operating as documents; checker built)

[0394] A manager register states "Nothing runs unmanaged" and lists a hierarchy (chief executive, vice presidents, directors, product managers, filled by virtual managers), each role with scope, reporting line, cadence, responsibilities, metrics and weekly deliverables; a portfolio register lists programs and products with manager, vision, a roadmap of at most 5 features (size S, M or L, goal, user, success metric, closing probe), projects with handoff documents and a program tracking issue. The weekly written update is due Monday 9am as a comment on the tracking issue; escalation targets are 4 hours (product manager), 24 hours (director), 24 hours (vice president), immediate (chief executive, severe). In the running system the managers are personas whose hourly decisions are shown at `/managers` (section 47.1). The register dated 2026-11-07 shows 2 of 12 products with an assigned manager and ten marked to-be-assigned with a due date of 2026-11-14.

[0395] A compliance script parses the register table `| Product | Manager | Director | VP | Status |` and the portfolio headings `### Product: `, computes `hasManager`, `inPortfolio`, `hasDirector`, `hasVP`, `isLive`, treats a missing manager or director as a blocking issue, a missing portfolio entry as a warning, a live product without management as critical, and exits with code 1 when blocking issues exist so a job can fail. Observation: `isLive` is inside the conjunction for "fully compliant", so a planned product with every assignment would not be labelled compliant; the weekly-update and roadmap-issue checks named in its header are not implemented in its body. Status: built in the reference implementation; prophetic for the missing checks.

### 50.2 Status reports and owner notifications (built, partially operating)

[0396] A generator writes `reports/YYYY-MM-DD-status.md` (executive summary, metrics table with target, actual and status, per-product sections, weekly-update compliance table, blockers, escalations, milestones, action items, recommendations, next report date seven days ahead). The 2026-11-07 report shows 12 products, 2 live, "Products with Managers 17% (2/12)" below target. The values are constants in the script and the colour is not computed, so it is a template; a ledger-driven embodiment computes manager coverage from the checker, update timeliness from comment timestamps against Monday 9am, blockers from labels, uptime from probes and the overall colour as the worst metric colour (prophetic). Digests and notices in the running system: the "what changed" feed of section 47.1; the realtime digest whose revision moves with the owner count; a weekly scorecard digest scheduled Monday 07:30 and a subscription digest with double opt-in and one-click unsubscribe. A code email's delivery status is recorded in `owner_codes.sent_ok`. Grouped new-owner-item notifications with a dedupe key, a daily owner digest and a failed-send log for them are labelled prophetic (not yet built) here, and the hourly UX auditor files one deduplicated request per product per day as the nearest built analogue. Status: built, partially operating.

### 50.3 Disclosure and voice guards (built, partially operating)

[0397] A principal-voice guard (run daily and throttled by a ledger row per day) checks outbound posts made in the principal's name against a configured list of forbidden patterns and a cadence cap, and files an issue naming the violations. A recurring fetch of served pages that fails on a list of forbidden terms is the prophetic extension: a job lists page URLs, fetches each, matches a term list, and files a deduplicated issue naming the page and term, with the list and URLs held outside the code. Status: owner-voice guard built and operating in the reference implementation; page-term guard prophetic.

### 50.4 Handoff documents and close-your-loops rules (built and operating)

[0398] A shared agent protocol held in one hub file is merged into each repository between `fleet-protocol:start` and `fleet-protocol:end` markers. Steps: read the newest `docs/handoff/` file and open issues labelled `agent-ready`, comment `claim: <agent> <date>`; work on `agent/<issue#>-<slug>` with a draft pull request; update `docs/handoff/<slug>.md` from a template after each stage (state, decisions, next step, how to verify); on low budget push and comment `released: <agent>, next: <step>`; finish with tests green, a live probe and `status: done`. Labels: `agent-ready`, `claimed`, `needs-review`, `blocked:<reason>`. The issue format has problem, context, files, acceptance criteria, test plan, closing probe, size, `front:` and `back:` lines. Agents do not review their own pull requests, and auto-merge is allowed only when checks are green. Every item an agent opens is a loop with an principal (an agent, never the person), a fix, a closing probe and evidence, recorded as an issue labelled `loop`; an audit script run every 6 hours files `fleet-audit` issues, closes them with evidence and flags a `loop` issue lacking `closing probe:`. Status: built and operating in the reference implementation.

### 50.5 Attribution of model calls and actions (built and operating)

[0399] A wrapper around the model binding upserts into `ai_call_counters (day, worker, purpose, model, calls, errors, in_chars, ms, in_tok, out_tok, neurons)` with primary key `(day, worker, purpose, model)`, taking tokens from the response usage or `in_chars/4`, and `neurons = (in_tok*rate_in + out_tok*rate_out)/10^6` from a per-model table. Worked example with rates 1,818 and 6,545: 2,000 input and 300 output tokens give (3,636,000 + 1,963,500)/10^6 = 5.5995 neurons. A failure never changes the call. Actions are attributed by `human_responses.credential`, `human_actions.resolution` text naming the credential, and `cmd_log.owner`. Status: built and operating in the reference implementation.

## 51. Ultrastable instance and further small mechanisms

### 51.1 The Ultrastable instance (site embodiment built; dashboard embodiment unverified)

[0400] "Ultrastable instance" means an installation of the organization kit (section 19) whose managed organization is the Ultrastable product company, with an owner console constructed as in sections 47 to 50. In one embodiment a site worker serves pages including a home page, an explanation page, a proof page and a pilot page, a health route, and a proof section read at request time from a claims interface with fail-soft fallback text; a test asserts the redirects, at most one primary action per page, canonical links, the 404 and the offline fallback; a declaration lists post-deploy probes with mode `revert`. A dashboard embodiment of Table 51-1 is described; it is prophetic as to the features listed.

| Feature | Ultrastable domain served | Source tables |
|---|---|---|
| One-view status | core, site, pilot, patent, policy pack | program and product registers, probes |
| Owner cards and one-tap options | entity, trademark, domain, signing | `human_actions`, `human_responses` |
| One-tap questions | naming, buyers, pilot terms, credit | `owner_asks`, `product_profile` |
| Emergency pause | stop of all agent dispatch | control authority with revision |
| Proof page | product claims | claims ledger, scored predictions |

[0401] Portable hosting. Edge: a dispatcher worker with hostname and path routing (unauthenticated hosts serve pages; the console path serves assets and `/api/*` behind the code sign-in), a relational database binding, a service binding to the control authority and a scheduled trigger. Container: one image of the same router on standard request and response interfaces, environment variables for the owner address and salt. GCP Cloud Run embodiment (prophetic): one service for the router and one for the control authority with service-to-service identity tokens replacing binding properties, a managed database, Cloud Scheduler for ticks, a secret manager for the salt, and an email-sending service for codes; an unreachable authority still fails closed. Status: prophetic (not yet built) for the Cloud Run variant and the container image.

### 51.2 Register of further mechanisms (each with its own status)

| # | Mechanism | Status |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Audit rows with credential; undo of agent pull requests; SQL backups before UPDATE/DELETE | Built and operating; general inverse for every verb prophetic |
| 2 | Notifications: grouped new items, daily digest, dedupe key, failed-send log | Changes feed and realtime digest built; grouped email notices prophetic |
| 3 | Disclosure guard | Principal-voice guard built; page-term fetch guard prophetic |
| 4 | Release notes one per line below the version line so a bump is a one-line patch; a conflict-repair script | Built in the reference implementation |

[0402] Technical effect of the group. The principal acts only on identity-bound items by taps, reversible work moves to agents in one tap without a code, every state-changing action is bound to a recorded credential, and the system fails closed when uncertain. Measurable quantities: open cards without an identity-bound line (target zero), the count of owner items versus fleet-handled items, the watchmaker index, and time from card creation to probe-observed closure.

## 52. Self-description record set and honest self-knowledge

[0403] "Self-description" means a machine-readable and human-readable document set, produced by an organization's own system, that states the system's capabilities, limits, access boundaries, governance and current state. "Self-description record set" means the typed records from which the self-description is rendered. "Evidence" for a statement means a probe, a ledger query, a commit or a measured reading that supports it, recorded with the time it was taken. "Honest-gap list" means the records of what the system cannot do, has not verified, or has only described. "Committed copy" means the rendering of the self-description stored in the organization's repository. These terms are used identically below. Terms of section 1 and the registers of sections 18 and 23 to 25 are used as defined there.

[0404] Technical problem. An autonomous system that changes itself accumulates three kinds of drift: the text that describes it (instruction files, handoff notes, status pages) diverges from the running system; a new agent session or a new tenant starts without knowing what it may do, what exists and what is broken; and statements of ability are asserted without checks, so that a system can "know" it can do something it has never done. Statements about what a system can do, written by the system or by its agents, are themselves outputs that can be false. The mechanisms below give each statement a typed record, a probe or an evidence reference, and a generation loop that compares the rendered text with the running system and with the repository.

### 52.1 Record set

[0405] In one embodiment the self-description record set comprises the following records. Several already exist as registers in the kernel of section 23. The others are described as embodiments.

| Record | Fields (illustrative) | Source of truth | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| `capability_ledger` | capability, acquired_by (construction, delegation), evidence_ref, acquired_at | kernel registry | built and operating (kernel table 2026-10-12) |
| `belief_registry` | belief, probe_sql, expected, last_observed, last_checked, falsified_at | kernel registry; probes named `belief-*` | built and operating |
| `vocabulary_register` | term, definition, propagated_to_fleet | kernel registry | built and operating |
| `objective_space` | objective, tier (ratified, derived, novel), probation_until | section 31 | built and operating |
| `spof_register` | failure, halts_what, mitigation, detector | kernel registry | built and operating |
| `component_advertisement` | component, routes, capabilities, probes, principal | section 18 and worker contract of section 27.2 | built, partially operating (a census found workers lacking the contract) |
| `product_boundary` | worker, class (core, app, plane), role, entangled | boundary file and its check, section 44.2 | built |
| `autonomy_policy` | surfaces, ceilings, rungs, action flags, harness deny rules | policy file and its validator | built (tested offline; section 19A) |
| `standing_limits` | limit, wins_over, recorded_refusal_rule | policy file | built as data |
| `access_map` | surface, credential_name, present (true or false), probe, checked_at; never a value | bootstrapper stage `detect` | built for presence detection at the seed (offline tested) |
| `known_gaps` and honest-gap list | gap, observed_how, principal, closing_probe, opened_at | this section | built as a prose document only; typed records prophetic |
| `product_claims` | id, claim, probe_sql, evidence_note | section 44.3 | built and operating |

[0406] Status: the registers marked built are operating in one implementation; the single typed `self_description` view over them, the typed honest-gap table and the generator of section 53 are prophetic (not yet built). A first hand-written self-awareness record exists as a document of eight sections (what the system is, bootstrap path, observed capabilities, observed limits with the path taken, the interrupt gate, owner-only items, self-correction, known gaps), written on 2026-11-10 and amended by reviewed change. It is not generated from the ledgers.

### 52.2 Statement classes

[0407] Each statement in the self-description has a class that determines the evidence it needs. Class A, "measured": a quantity or a state (for example a count of live workers, a latest tick time); evidence is a probe result no older than a freshness limit (for example 3 hours for a ledger refresh, 2 hours for ticks, as in the claim probes of section 44.3). Class B, "structural": a statement about code, files or policy (for example "every live worker is classified"); evidence is the result of a deterministic check (for example the boundary check, which fails on an unclassified or non-live classified worker) and the commit identifier at which it ran. Class C, "observed limit": the system tried and was refused or failed; evidence is the recorded refusal or failure (an issue, a refusal row, a log line) and the path taken. Class D, "prophetic": a statement of something not built; evidence is the absence of a probe, and the statement must carry the label "prophetic" and an principal. A statement lacking a class or lacking the evidence its class requires is a defect handled by the consistency check of 53.3.

[0408] Observed limits are the most useful to a successor session and are rarely recorded. Examples of observed limits in one implementation: sessions could not create repositories or organizations, so per-repository layouts were kept as folders and git bundles; a repository was usable only after it was attached and the hosting platform's application had access; a project board required an interface sessions could not call, so milestones and labelled issues served as the roadmap view; a permission classifier denied closing and commenting on pull requests in one session and the session stated the denial and left the items open for the principal or another session, without rewording the action to pass the same check; a connector sign-in expired and a reconnect card was requested; chats can be deleted, so each result was committed in the turn that produced it. Status: built as a record and operating as a practice in the reference implementation. Technical effect: a successor does not retry a refused action, spends no attempts rediscovering a limit, and sees which limits are standing (policy) and which are environmental (current credentials).

### 52.3 Capability, belief and probe records

[0409] A capability enters `capability_ledger` only with `acquired_by` set to "construction" (the system built or deployed it) or "delegation" (the fleet performed it), and with an `evidence_ref` that resolves to a commit, a probe row or a ledger row. A capability that has been described but never exercised is stored with `evidence_ref` empty and rendered under the honest-gap list as "described, not exercised". Example, from the seed: the first real application of provisioning against a cloud account has not been run end to end; the bootstrapper's dry run, the tick logic and the policy validator are tested offline. The self-description therefore lists "provision a fresh cloud account" as described, not exercised, and a successor sees this when it loads the record. Status: the capability ledger is built and operating; the exercised-versus-described split is prophetic.

[0410] A belief is a statement the system relies on about itself or its environment, stored with a falsifying probe: a read-only query whose result contradicts the belief when it fails. For example, a belief "ticks run at least every 10 minutes" has the probe `SELECT CASE WHEN COUNT(*) > 0 THEN 'ok' ELSE 'no tick in 2 hours' END FROM tick_runs WHERE started_at > datetime('now','-2 hours')`. When a probe returns other than `ok`, the belief is marked falsified with a timestamp, a work item is opened by the contract runner, and the self-description renders the belief as falsified until a later probe passes. Status: built and operating for beliefs in the kernel registry; the rendering into a single document is prophetic.

### 52.4 Access map: presence, never values

[0411] The access map lists each surface the system could reach (repository host, runtime account, work tracker, model interface, chat webhook, outreach, domain, payment, legal) with the name of the credential or binding that grants it (for example an environment variable name or a secret-store binding name), a boolean `present`, the probe that determined presence (for example a check that the variable is set, not its content), and the time. The stage `detect` of the bootstrapper (step 1504) already records which connector credentials and tools exist and never records values; each missing required credential becomes a gate card whose closing probe is `env:NAME`. Status: built for detection at the seed, tested offline; the persistent `access_map` table and its refresh are prophetic. The renderer refuses to write any field whose key matches a secret-name pattern and whose value is not a boolean, and a repository scan step in the checks workflow (private keys, token prefixes) is a second barrier. Technical effect: an agent can learn that a surface is unreachable (and so open a card for the principal) without any process handling a credential.

### 52.5 Autonomy policy, ceilings and limits as data

[0412] The self-description embeds, by reference to the policy file and not by copy, the surfaces with their permission ceilings (none, propose, execute), starting and current rungs, action flags `reversible` and `identity_bound`, and the harness deny rules that mirror gated actions (section 19A, paragraph 0078d to 0078f; evidence: autonomy-policy file with surfaces repo, runtime, work tracker, internal chat, outreach and others). It also embeds the standing limits that outrank trust: the agent harness's permission checks, classifier and safety refusals, the secret lock, consent and opt-out rules for outreach, the principal's override and another session's live claim. A session that loads the description derives, for a proposed action, the tuple (surface, action class, ceiling, current rung, gate state) by lookup, so that "may I merge this" is answered from data and not from recollection. Where the harness's own checks differ from the policy, the checks win and the description says so. Status: built as data and as a mirrored-deny consistency test; the derived lookup as a single function is prophetic.

### 52.6 Product boundary classification

[0413] The self-description states which parts of the managed system are core (the product), app (an application running on it) or plane (a separated plane that never ships), by reading the boundary file. In one implementation the file of 2026-11-10 lists 40 workers (20 core, 12 app, 8 plane) and the check fails when a live worker is in no class (section 44.2). The description adds two derived lines: the list of entangled workers (workers whose core and application logic share a file), and the count of live workers not classified, expected zero. Status: built (check and manifest); the derived lines are produced by the check's JSON mode and are built.

### 52.7 Known gaps and honest-gap list

[0414] The honest-gap list is ordered by consequence. Entries from the first record include: the first cloud provisioning of the seed has not run end to end; the system's measured decision accuracy is scored by the system itself and has no outside audit; several sessions worked in parallel on the first day and duplicated effort, for which claims and handoff files are the response; a security overview lists per-tenant isolation as not built, dashboard authentication as not built (later built in the owner console, section 48), no outside review of the policy and trust numbers self-scored. Each entry has an principal (a role or the principal), an observed-how, and a closing probe, which is the probe that would pass when the gap closes (for example, a successful tick count of six within an hour after provisioning, as in the seed's kit item that closes only on six complete ticks in an hour). A gap whose closing probe cannot be stated is recorded as "unprobeable" and shown to the principal in a card.

[0415] Technical effect of the group. Statements about the system carry their own evidence or their own label. In one implementation the first record exposed four facts that would otherwise have been asserted without checks: the licence status of a code repository (several documents misstated it; the repository carried a licence file, and a reviewed change corrected the documents), the absence of a project board, the absence of end-to-end provisioning and the limits on repository creation. Measurable quantities: count of statements without evidence (target zero), count of falsified beliefs and their age, count of gaps without a closing probe, and the count of corrections of self-description errors found by an outside reader per month (a measure of error escape; not measured).

## 53. Generation, consistency check and amendment of the self-description (FIG. 30)

[0416] Status: prophetic as a whole procedure (not yet built). Its parts have operating counterparts that are identified in each step: the hourly index workflow, which regenerates two repository documents from the issue tracker and commits only when they changed; the boundary check; the claims-as-probes pages; the mirrored-deny test; the verify script in the checks workflow, which reports drift as an advisory warning. The procedure unifies these into one loop. Where the worker contract of section 27.2 compares deployed source with repository source byte for byte (paragraph 0158), the loop here compares the rendered description with the repository copy.

### 53.1 Generation loop

[0417] "Generator" means a program run by the organization's scheduler that performs steps 3002 to 3022. In one embodiment it runs hourly, on merge to the default branch, and on a ledger change event (for example a failure-mode class changing state), and it is idempotent: running it twice in a row produces an empty diff the second time.

[0418] Step 3002: trigger. The generator starts from the scheduler, a repository merge event or a change event. It takes a lock keyed by "self-description" so that two runs cannot write at once (the index workflow uses a concurrency group with cancel-in-progress false for the same purpose).

[0419] Step 3004: read. The generator reads the live ledgers: capability ledger, belief registry, vocabulary register, objective space, SPOF register, trust ledger summary (by surface and action class, with rung), product boundary (by running the boundary check in JSON mode), policy file, component advertisements and the failure-mode register summary (counts by class and state). Reads are read-only.

[0420] Step 3006: probe. For each record with a probe (beliefs, product claims, access map presence, gap closing probes) the generator runs the probe through the same read-only probe runner used by remediation contracts (sections 41 and 44.3), which refuses any statement not a single SELECT. It records `observed`, `detail` and `checked_at`. A probe that errors is recorded as `error`, and an `error` statement is rendered as "unverified at <time>" and never as passing.

[0421] Step 3008: assemble and render.
